#10349. Google Fi app redesign brings floating toolbar & Ask Fi

Google Fi Wireless is the latest first-party app to adopt a floating toolbar as part of a small navigation redesign.

Previously, the companion app still used a tall Material 3 bottom bar. With the network switching update announced last week, Google Fi now has a floating navigation toolbar.

The tabs remain Home, Plan, Billing, Shop, and Support. A pill-shaped tab indicator is leveraged, while there’s a prominent shadow effect. Given the floating nature, you see what’s around the toolbar, while Fi Wireless now extends underneath the system navigation bar.

However, the Shop tab loads a webpage as before, resulting in a gap and wasted space.

There’s now a floating action button for the new Google AI-powered Ask Fi chatbot. It provides “instant account help and troubleshooting.” If you ask for human support, the contents of the conversation carry over.

This redesign is rolling out with version 171 of the app as a server-side update. Google Fi joins Google Photos, Chat, and Pixel Now Playing in adopting floating navigation, which is likely the new first-party mandate for a more edge-to-edge design.

Secondary network switching is not yet widely available, with Google telling us last week that it’s a gradual rollout. The MVNO is also ending Pixel exclusivity for Wi-Fi Auto Connect+ with availability on “select” Samsung and Motorola phones.

#10343. How to Build a Unified Lead Tracking Pipeline for Web Forms and Phone Calls

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A visitor clicks a search ad, submits a quote request, and calls your sales team ten minutes later. The form integration creates a CRM contact. The phone platform records a call. By Friday, the marketing report shows two leads, while sales remembers one conversation that turned into a deal.

Both systems captured something useful. The problem is that neither record explains how the two interactions belong together.

A unified tracking pipeline gives forms and calls a common event format, preserves their acquisition context, and connects them to CRM outcomes. It also needs to survive a less tidy reality: repeated callbacks, missing campaign data, delayed updates, and a CRM that occasionally refuses requests.

This tutorial builds the core of that pipeline with JavaScript and PostgreSQL. We will capture campaign parameters, define a shared event contract, and persist events alongside the work needed to deliver them. Provider authentication and CRM requests belong in adapters around this core, using the documentation for the services you choose.

Before writing an endpoint, decide what you are counting. A phone call can produce several status callbacks. A person can submit two forms. An existing customer can contact sales about a new purchase.

Use separate identifiers for these different things:

This separation prevents two common mistakes. Deduplicating by call ID alone can discard later status updates. Counting every callback as a lead inflates demand.

For this implementation, each event is unique within a tenant and source. A tenant is the business whose data you are processing; the source identifies a website or provider account. The same provider event ID can therefore appear in two accounts without causing a collision.

Keep the original interactions even after you connect them to one contact. The form and the call are both useful evidence. Your reporting layer decides whether they represent one opportunity.

Campaign parameters are easiest to capture when the visitor first arrives. Waiting until the form submission can lose them if the visitor has already moved to another page.

Google's campaign URL documentation explains the roles of utm_source, utm_medium, and utm_campaign. The following helper reads those fields and a few optional ones, without copying the entire query string:

const params = new URL(url).searchParams;

"utm_source", "utm_medium", "utm_campaign",

return Object.fromEntries(keys.flatMap(key => {

The browser's URLSearchParams interface handles query parsing and decoding. It saves you from writing string-splitting code that breaks on encoded characters.

Run this helper through your site's existing consent and attribution flow. Where storage is permitted, associate the result with an opaque attribution reference in your backend and retain that reference through the visit. A later form submission sends the reference; the server resolves it within the correct tenant and visitor session. The form must still work when attribution storage is unavailable.

Keep first-touch and current-touch records separately if you need both. Overwriting the first campaign every time someone returns makes the eventual report answer a different question.

Treat campaign labels as untrusted input. Visitors can edit query strings and hidden fields. Validate them on the server, escape them when displayed, and never use them as authorization or as the sole evidence for commission payments. Avoid putting names, email addresses, or other personal information in UTM values.

Missing tags also need careful handling. An empty UTM object only tells you that no accepted UTM values were captured. Classifying a visit as direct, organic, or referral requires additional evidence and an explicit attribution rule.

Forms carry browser context naturally. Phone calls need a bridge between the number dialed and the marketing interaction that prompted the call.

There are two useful levels of precision. A number assigned to one campaign can identify that campaign. Dynamic number insertion can support a more detailed association by displaying tracking numbers according to a visitor or session mapping. That association depends on the provider's number allocation and expiry rules.

Providers differ in how they implement this, and in what they expose beyond it, such as keyword-level reporting or conversation analysis. If you are still choosing one, this comparison of attribution software with call tracking is a useful starting point. At the integration boundary, check which event identifiers, attribution fields, delivery mechanisms, and authentication options your configured product exposes.

Keep that provider-specific work in an adapter. Its job is to turn an authenticated notification into your event contract and resolve any attribution reference supported by the provider's data. The application schema below is an internal contract; each provider adapter translates its documented payload into that contract.

Store the basis of a match with the attribution record. For example, session_match, campaign_number, and unknown make different levels of evidence visible to reporting. If a number has been reassigned, use the mapping valid when the call occurred, not whichever mapping happens to be current when the callback arrives. If the evidence is ambiguous, leave the association unresolved.

Caller ID is a separate issue. A shared office number, a withheld number, or an outdated contact record can make automatic identity matching unreliable. A confirmed CRM contact ID or an authenticated account provides a stronger link. Treat a matching phone number as a candidate for reconciliation rather than proof that two records belong to the same person.

A shared event envelope lets the rest of your application work with a small, predictable vocabulary. Here is an illustrative call event:

"occurred_at": "2026-11-06T10:14:00.000Z",

The event ID identifies this occurrence. The interaction ID groups it with other events for the same call. contact_ref can remain empty until you have enough evidence to connect the interaction to a contact.

Keep contact details in a controlled contact store and campaign details in an attribution store. The references above point to those records; they are not credentials and must not grant access on their own.

The following factory checks the shared envelope. Each adapter still needs its own validation for provider fields and the contents of data.

"form.submitted", "call.started", "call.completed",

export function makeEvent(input, trusted) {

const ids = [trusted.tenantId, trusted.source,

if (ids.some(id => typeof id !== "string" ||

throw new TypeError("Invalid event identifiers");

throw new TypeError("Unsupported event type");

const time = Date.parse(input.occurredAt);

if (typeof input.occurredAt !== "string" ||

new Date(time).toISOString() !== input.occurredAt) {

throw new TypeError("Expected a canonical UTC timestamp");

attribution_ref: input.attributionRef ?? null,

Resolve trusted from server configuration and the authenticated integration. Never let a submitted tenant_id choose whose records a webhook can write. Resolve contact and attribution references within that same tenant.

For a form, issue a submission ID and reuse it when retrying the same submission. Generate a new one when the visitor deliberately starts another inquiry. Preserve the originally accepted timestamp as well. For a phone event, prefer the provider's stable event ID. If the provider documents a sequence number, a call ID plus that sequence can identify an occurrence within a provider account.

Twilio's call resource documentation provides a concrete example: call progress callbacks include a SequenceNumber, and separate HTTP requests can arrive out of order. Preserve that sequence in the adapter. A late “ringing” event should remain in the history without replacing a newer terminal state.

Authenticate before calling this factory. Use the provider's supported validator and the exact request representation it requires. Twilio's webhook security guide describes signature validation, including the importance of the request URL and payload. Other providers may use different mechanisms. Public form endpoints need their own input limits, abuse controls, and appropriate CSRF protection.

Consider a failure between these two operations: the application saves an event, then crashes before scheduling its CRM update. The event exists, but nothing is waiting to deliver it.

A transactional outbox addresses that gap. Save the event and a delivery task in the same database transaction, then let a worker process the task. AWS explains this dual-write problem in its transactional outbox guidance.

Here is a compact schema. received_at records ingestion time, while the envelope retains the event's original occurred_at value.

received_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),

PRIMARY KEY (tenant_id, source, event_id)

id bigint GENERATED ALWAYS AS IDENTITY PRIMARY KEY,

available_at timestamptz NOT NULL DEFAULT now(),

FOREIGN KEY (tenant_id, source, event_id)

REFERENCES lead_events (tenant_id, source, event_id)

The ingestion function below accepts a pg pool and a normalized event. Install pg in your Node.js project, save the function as persist.mjs, and initialize the pool using your application's database configuration.

export async function persistEvent(pool, event) {

const key = [event.tenant_id, event.source, event.event_id];

const values = [...key, JSON.stringify(event)];

await client.query("BEGIN ISOLATION LEVEL READ COMMITTED");

ON CONFLICT (tenant_id, source, event_id) DO NOTHING

WHERE tenant_id = $1 AND source = $2 AND event_id = $3

const error = new Error("Event ID reused with new data");

INSERT INTO crm_outbox (tenant_id, source, event_id)

return { inserted: inserted.rowCount === 1 };

PostgreSQL's INSERT documentation defines the conflict handling used here. The unique key makes duplicate detection hold across application processes. A duplicate with the same normalized payload creates no second task; reuse of the key with different data raises a conflict for investigation.

This depends on immutable event bodies. Do not add a fresh receipt timestamp, random identifier, or current CRM status to the normalized payload on each delivery. Store later enrichment separately or emit a new event. Otherwise an ordinary retry will appear to be a conflicting event.

The function deliberately uses one database client throughout the transaction, as required by the node-postgres transaction documentation. Parameter values are passed separately from SQL, following its parameterized query guidance.

After this function commits, acknowledge the notification using the response expected by the sender. An identical duplicate should also receive a successful acknowledgement. If persistence fails, return the appropriate failure response and rely on the configured retry or reconciliation process. A success response sent before durable storage can turn a temporary database failure into a missing event.

For voice integrations, distinguish an informational status callback from a request asking your application how to handle a live call. They can require different response bodies, as explained in the Twilio webhooks FAQ.

The outbox gives you durable work to process. It does not make a remote CRM operation happen exactly once.

Imagine the CRM saves an update, but the worker crashes before setting processed_at. When the task runs again, the same request may reach the CRM twice. Design for that case explicitly.

Use a stable external identifier for each contact, opportunity, and activity, and check which deduplication guarantees the destination supports. HubSpot's contacts API documentation, for example, describes upserts using unique properties. Support for a contact upsert does not automatically make every associated activity or workflow idempotent. Verify the behavior of each operation you send.

If the destination cannot deduplicate a create request and a timeout leaves its result uncertain, reconcile that operation before repeating it. A local “sent” flag alone cannot prove whether the remote write happened.

The worker should claim due tasks, commit a recoverable lease, and perform network calls outside the database transaction. A lease allows another worker to recover work after a crash. When multiple workers poll the outbox, PostgreSQL's SKIP LOCKED behavior can help claim separate rows, but the lease and its recovery logic still need to be implemented.

Retry transient failures with increasing delays and jitter. Respect the destination's rate-limit instructions. Send persistent validation failures to a review queue with a useful error category, and provide a way to replay them after correction. The schema above is the ingestion foundation; a production worker needs lease fields, failure details, and a retry policy appropriate to its destination.

Also preserve ordering where it changes meaning. If two events update the same contact or opportunity, process them serially or reject stale updates using a stored version. A historical event should not move a won opportunity back to an earlier stage.

Once both channels reach the CRM, connect them to the result the business cares about. A form submission is an inquiry. A completed phone call is a technical outcome. Neither alone establishes that a lead qualified or a purchase happened. If agents record call dispositions, such as whether a call was qualified or transferred to a closer, that field is a richer signal than call completion status alone.

Have the CRM or the system that owns the transaction emit explicit outcome events, such as lead.qualified and deal.won. Include the internal opportunity ID and the original outcome time. Model reversals or cancellations explicitly if they affect revenue reporting.

Then choose the unit behind each metric. For a report about opportunities acquired during a particular month, a qualification rate can use qualified opportunities divided by all opportunities in that acquisition cohort. Counting qualification events received during the month would answer a different question and could include older inquiries.

Deduplicate opportunities before calculating channel totals. A person who submits a form and calls about the same purchase should retain both interactions, while the opportunity receives credit according to your chosen attribution rule. Label that rule in the report so first-touch and last-touch numbers are not compared as though they measure the same thing.

Measure data quality alongside performance. Track unresolved attribution, duplicate deliveries, ingestion failures, and the age of the oldest pending outbox task. Once several sources and destinations feed the same reports, a data observability platform can watch these signals across the whole pipeline instead of relying on checks inside a single worker. An apparently quiet campaign may actually have a broken integration.

Operational logs should contain enough context to investigate failures without becoming another customer database. Use internal references and error categories, and avoid dumping webhook bodies into logs. The OWASP Logging Cheat Sheet provides guidance on excluding or protecting sensitive values.

A successful form submission proves little about retries or missing data. Before connecting production traffic, test the cases that could distort reporting or lose an inquiry.

Run database cases against PostgreSQL with separate connections so the tests exercise actual constraints and transaction behavior. Use the provider's test tools or signed fixtures for webhook authentication. Test CRM retries against a sandbox with the destination's real duplicate-handling rules.

Start rollout with one form and one phone source. Trace a known inquiry from its captured campaign through its interactions to its CRM outcome. Reconcile event totals against the source systems over the same time window, allowing for delivery delays. Expand once that path is explainable and failures are visible.

The same adapter pattern extends to inquiries that start offline, such as leads collected at a trade show and exported as a CSV or XLSX file. Import each row as a form.submitted event with the event as its source, a stable row identifier as event_id, and the original capture time as occurred_at. Because the outbox treats an identical event as a duplicate, re-importing the same export won’t create a second CRM task.

What is a unified lead tracking pipeline?

It is a workflow that converts inquiries from different channels into a shared data model, preserves their acquisition context, and connects them to business outcomes. Forms and calls keep their own interaction records, while contacts and opportunities provide the relationships needed for reporting.

Can I connect a phone call to a website visit?

Sometimes. Dynamic number insertion can provide a session or visitor association when the provider's mapping is available and valid. A campaign-specific number usually supports campaign-level attribution. If the caller uses a saved number, switches devices, or calls after a mapping expires, the available evidence may be less precise.

Handle duplicate delivery and identity resolution separately. Stable event IDs and a database constraint prevent repeated notifications from producing repeated work. Connecting several interactions to one contact or opportunity requires a separate matching policy, with ambiguous cases left unresolved until there is enough evidence.

Should a completed call count as a conversion?

Only if that is the conversion your report explicitly measures. A completed call does not establish that a prospect qualified or bought something. Keep technical call status separate from CRM outcomes and use the latter when reporting qualified demand or revenue.

Do I need a message broker for this architecture?

You can begin with PostgreSQL and an outbox worker if that setup meets your throughput and latency needs. A broker becomes useful when several consumers need independent delivery, or when buffering and scale justify the additional operations. In either case, retries still require idempotent consumers.

What happens when tracking consent is unavailable?

Keep the inquiry flow usable and apply your site's existing consent rules to optional attribution collection and storage. Accept permitted form or call data, leave unavailable attribution empty, and avoid reconstructing a visitor identity through fingerprinting. Reports should expose that missing context instead of inventing a campaign match.

#10336. Securing FastAPI Applications: JWT Authentication and Rate Limiting with Redis

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Building a modern web API is only half the battle. Ensuring it is secure, resilient, and capable of handling malicious traffic is what separates a hobby project from a production-ready service. When building highly scalable applications, we typically lean on stateless architectures. However, features like token revocation and rate limiting require state.

In this guide, we will explore the architectural concepts behind building a secure API using FastAPI, Redis, JSON Web Tokens (JWT), and SlowAPI. We will break down the theory, the tech stack, and the flow of data—without getting bogged down in the code.

Before diving into the architecture, it is crucial to understand the role each tool plays in our security model:

Security starts with how you manage your secrets. The first step in our architectural journey is setting up a configuration file and a local environment file.

Hardcoding secret keys, database URLs, or algorithm types directly into your application is a severe security risk. Instead, we load these from environment variables. The application uses a settings manager to read variables like the Redis host, port, the JWT secret key, the algorithm (typically HS256), and the token expiration time. This ensures that sensitive data stays out of your version control system.

To handle rate limiting and token tracking, we need a connection to Redis. Because FastAPI is inherently asynchronous, we initialize an asynchronous Redis client.

Redis acts as the central state-holder for our API. By using Redis, we ensure that even if our API is running on five different servers behind a load balancer, the rate limits and the token blacklists remain globally synchronized.

Next, we establish the utility functions that handle the cryptographic heavy lifting. This step is divided into two main parts:

Here, we initialize the FastAPI application and attach slowapi.

slowapi needs a way to identify the client making the request. By default, it uses the client's IP address. We attach the limiter to the application state and register an exception handler. This ensures that if a user breaches the rate limit, the API gracefully returns a "429 Too Many Requests" error instead of crashing.

We also set up a dependency that acts as a security guard for our protected routes. This dependency intercepts incoming requests, extracts the token, and decodes it. It then checks Redis to see if the token has been blacklisted (for example, if the user logged out before the token expired). If the token is valid and not blacklisted, the request proceeds; otherwise, a "401 Unauthorized" error is thrown.

The login endpoint is where the magic happens. When a user submits their credentials, the API verifies the username and password. In a production scenario, this involves querying a database; in our example, we verify it against a hardcoded credential.

Once the credentials are validated, the API generates a JWT. To ensure we can invalidate this token later if needed, we store the token in Redis with a time-to-live (TTL) that matches the token's expiration time. Finally, the token is returned to the client, which will store it and send it in the Authorization header of future requests.

Finally, we create a route that requires both authentication and rate limiting.

Before the route's logic even executes, two things happen:

If the client passes both security checks, they are granted access to the highly secure data provided by the route.

To validate this system, you would conceptually walk through several test scenarios. First, you test the login flow with correct and incorrect credentials to ensure tokens are only issued to valid users.

Next, you test the rate limit by rapidly firing requests at the protected route to ensure the "429 Too Many Requests" response triggers. Finally, you test the security guard by trying to access the route with no token, an expired token, and a manually blacklisted token to ensure the API correctly denies access in all scenarios.

Building a secure API requires layering defenses. By combining FastAPI's speed, Redis's fast state management, JWT's stateless authentication, and slowapi's rate-limiting capabilities, you create a system that is both robust and scalable.

While this foundational text explains the core concepts, moving to production requires adding token refresh flows, implementing per-user rate limiting (rather than just IP-based), deploying Redis with high availability, and ensuring your secrets are managed by a secure vault. These practices will ensure your API remains secure as your user base grows.

#10333. Hardware hacker builds open-source portable 3.5-inch floppy Wi-Fi NAS

Have a ton of floppy disks with photos stashes somewhere? Take this with you and easily pull their data somewhere safer.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Hardware hacker BigCrimping (Crimp) has made a dedicated portable Wi-Fi NAS that allows for easily reading and copying 3.5" floppy disks over a network. The renaissance of vintage hardware also extends to some early digital cameras, like the Sony Mavica and Panasonic PV-SD families. These point-and-shooters were popular due to using bog-standard 3.5" floppies in PC format. Many folks may still have floppies from this era around, or simply want to breathe new life into their cameras.

The new device is called floppy3.5, and its design is completely open-source. Making one ought to be pretty easy, as Crimp made all the required files available on GitHub, including data for 3D printing the enclosure, and a custom PCB with an RP2354A microcontroller and an ESP32-S3 bridge. Besides those items, you'll need predictable necessities like a battery, a slimline floppy drive, a Wi-Fi module, and a microSD slot.

The onboard monochrome LCD display looks pretty handy, as Crimp made it show a bevy of information, including sector and cylinder counts, a good/bad sector map, file counts, and even a firmware update progress counter. The onboard buttons do what they say: READ to scan the files, or SAVE to dump them to an SD card. The DISP and POWER buttons return you to the home screen, and power the device off and on.

The card in said slot optionally stores the disk's files, allowing you to easily go around a storeroom or attic and just insert floppies one after another for archival without needing a computer. When it's time to manage content, you can access the floppy3.5's web interface over the network, and even connect to it directly with a USB cable if you need fancier features like fetching entire disk images.

Crimp chose to use the Greaseweazle floppy drive comms protocol instead of relying on a conventional floppy drive controller. Besides saving on hardware, this allows full control over the drive, as Greaseweazle manages a drive's wire signals directly, making for easy imaging and all sorts of direct data manipulation, regardless of a disk's underlying format. By default, floppy3.5 understands FAT12 for its main purpose, but you can use the Greaseweazle utilities over the cable if needed.

#10332. AI features could be turned off with a simple toggle, Canva proves

AI has taken over everything in the past few years, with AI features bombarding virtually every app or service available. If you’ve ever wanted to just turn off AI features, Canva proves that it could be a simple toggle, if companies actually wanted to give you a choice.

Canva today announced “AI Controls,” a new toggle that completely turns off generative AI features within the editing app.

Generative AI can be quite helpful in working on posters, editing images, and more, but it’s not everyone’s preference. Canva puts this well:

For some people, AI is the most exciting creative tool in a generation, while others prefer bringing their ideas to life without it. We believe the future of creativity should give everyone the freedom to choose their own path, whether that’s creating with AI, without it, or somewhere in between.

To give its users the choice, Canva’s Permissions menu now has a simple toggle for “Enable Generative AI features.” Toggling it off disables features such as Magic Write and Magic Edit. There’s also an option to limit how much AI-generated imagery appears in the Canva library.

AI has its place, but everyone should have a choice like this, especially when it comes to apps and services used for artistry in the same vein as Canva. Yet, the tech industry continues to go in the opposite direction, shoving AI into every corner whether it’s actually useful or not. Canva proves, though, that it doesn’t have to be that way.

With Grand Theft Auto VI set to finally launch tomorrow, November 19th, our long-awaited return to Vice City is nearly here. But after all this time, some of the gear you’ve used in San Andreas might feel out of place on the glamorous streets of Leonidas County. Luckily for you, we’ve gathered a few of our favorite Xbox and PlayStation 5 accessories to help your long gaming sessions run as smoothly as possible.

The 8BitDo Ultimate 3E — an upgraded pick for Xbox Series X and Series S consoles — is a very capable, comfortable controller with elite-level features, including a high polling rate and adjustable triggers. It’s on the expensive side at $149.99, but the customization and clean execution justify the cost. Read our review.

PlayStation 5 owners looking for a controller upgrade, meanwhile, might consider the DualSense Edge, Sony’s first-party pro-style controller. In addition to a variety of extra function buttons and paddles, the wireless gamepad sports adjustable trigger heights and swappable joysticks (in case the potentiometers start to drift). It’s a little bit heavier than Sony’s regular DualSense, and the battery life isn’t quite as good, but it offers customization you won’t find on the console’s default controller. Read our review.

Sony’s pro-style controller, the DualSense Edge, might suffer from shorter battery life than the standard DualSense model, but it features great software integration with the PlayStation 5 and plenty of customizable components.

Nothing’s worse than having to pause a heist so you can go hunt down a fresh set of AA batteries. The PowerA Duo Charging Station includes a rechargeable battery and replacement rear doors for two Xbox Series X / S or Xbox One controllers. Simply set them on the dock, and they’ll start charging.

PowerA’s PlayStation equivalent charges two DualSense Wireless Controllers simultaneously, with an included AC adapter, and comes with a two-year warranty.

The 2026 Roku Pro Series is the brand’s first OLED, and the 55-inch version retails for $999.99, which is on the low end of the price spectrum for OLED. Games feel richer and more immersive on OLED panels, thanks to their deeper black levels, which are the result of unlit pixels or sections of the screen. Each Pro Series panel also sports a 120Hz refresh rate, four HDMI 2.1 ports, and both low latency and variable refresh rates for gaming.

For a more premium offering, the Sony Bravia 8 II is one of our favorite screens for movies, with an engaging, immersive picture and excellent image processing. It has great gaming features, too, like a 120Hz refresh rate with variable refresh and an automatic low-latency mode. It’s not cheap, but the OLED makes for an excellent upgrade to your setup. Read our review.

Sony’s latest first-party headset, the $99 PlayStation Pulse Wireless, launches the same day as GTA VI. My colleague Cameron Faulkner recently went hands-on with the wireless headset ahead of its launch on November 19th, and came away impressed with its overall audio quality and comfort. The newest Pulse is also straightforward to set up and use, especially when paired with devices like the PlayStation Portal that support PlayStation Link, Sony’s proprietary low-latency audio connection. It also offers Bluetooth for use with other devices.

For Xbox gamers, it’s hard to beat the SteelSeries Arctis Nova 3X, which retails in multiple colors for $109.99 or less. The wireless headset is lightweight, comfortable, and compatible with a wide variety of other consoles and mobile devices as well, for those rare moments when you aren’t playing GTA VI.

If you’d rather the simplicity of a wired option, Sony’s InZone H6 Air skips the batteries and dongles, instead opting to plug straight into your controller. This open-back gaming headset is lightweight and comfortable, which is great for long hours and late nights. My colleague Cameron Faulkner described its sound as “spectacular,” with several equalizer options available for helping fine-tune the sound to your liking. Read our review.

Ikea’s Poäng chair balances affordability and comfort, with wide, supportive armrests that are just the right height for holding a controller. It comes in a variety of frame materials and cushion colors, too, making it easy to match your other furniture or console. This is my personal chair for console gaming, and I recently added a matching ottoman to the set for maximum relaxation.

#10329. Proton Pass is up to 60% off for Black Friday—and it does way more than just store your passwords

There’s a familiar interruption that comes with shopping online: you’ve found what you want, you’re ready to check out, and the store asks you to sign in. Was this the account with your usual password? Did you change it last time? Is it easier to reset it again?

Proton Pass is designed to take that friction out of your day. It creates strong, unique passwords, remembers them for you, and fills them in when you log in. But it also handles something a password alone can’t: keeping your real email address out of another retailer’s database.

For Black Friday, Proton Pass Plus and Pass Family are both 60% off their regular monthly pricing with a one-year subscription, while Proton Unlimited is 50% off. Pass Plus starts at $1.99 per month, billed annually, making this a good opportunity to get your passwords — and a few other important parts of your digital life — organized.

The problem with strong passwords isn’t understanding why you need them; it’s keeping track of a different one for every account.

Proton Pass does that work for you. When you sign up for a website, its password generator can create a strong password that’s completely unique. Save it in your vault, and Proton Pass can fill in your credentials when you return.

That’s useful whether you’re creating an account for a single purchase or signing into a service you use every day.

Unique passwords also help limit the fallout from a data breach. If a retailer exposes your login credentials, using a different password elsewhere means those same credentials won’t unlock your other accounts.

With unlimited logins and encrypted vaults that sync across devices, Proton Pass lets you keep those details together as you move between your phone and computer.

Proton Pass is available for practically every platform, from Chrome and Firefox on the web to native macOS, Windows, and Linux apps to, of course, iOS and Android. This makes it trivially easy to access your passwords, passkeys, and API credentials wherever you find yourself.

Black Friday shopping often leaves something behind long after the packages arrive: a steady stream of promotional emails from stores you barely remember visiting.

Proton Pass includes unlimited hide-my-email aliases, giving you an alternative to handing out your real address every time you create an account.

An alias is an extra email address that forwards messages to your normal inbox. You can give it to a shopping website and still receive your order confirmations and delivery updates, without sharing the underlying address you use for everything else.

If that alias starts receiving spam, you can switch it off. Your real email address stays the same, and you don’t have to update all your other accounts.

It’s a practical way to put some distance between your primary inbox and the websites you’re trying for the first time. A separate alias for each service also gives you more control: you can stop messages arriving through one address without disrupting the others.

Passwords are only part of what’s worth keeping in a secure, accessible place.

Proton Pass also stores notes, credit card details, and other confidential information in end-to-end encrypted vaults. That makes it useful for the small, everyday moments when you need something sensitive and don’t want to go looking for it.

When you’re booking a flight, saved passport details let you look up the information you need without digging through a drawer halfway through the reservation.

When you’re shopping online, saved card details mean you don’t have to leave the checkout page to find your wallet.

When you’re traveling, keeping a digital copy of an important document within reach gives you a convenient reference while you’re away from home. It doesn’t replace an original document where one is required, but it can save you from searching through scattered files and messages.

Pass Plus also includes a built-in two-factor authenticator, Dark Web Monitoring, and secure link and vault sharing. Those features give you more ways to manage access to your accounts and share sensitive information when you need to.

Pass Plus is the straightforward choice for one person who wants password generation, autofill, unlimited aliases, and secure storage.

Pass Family includes six Pass Plus accounts and a family admin panel. If several people in your household need a password manager, it’s worth considering rather than setting everyone up with separate subscriptions.

Proton Unlimited bundles Pass with Proton VPN, Proton Mail, Proton Drive, and more, including 500 GB of cloud storage. It’s the option to consider if you also want a VPN, encrypted email, and cloud storage alongside your password manager.

A password manager isn’t the most conspicuous Black Friday purchase, but it’s one you can put to work immediately: the next account you create, the next password you forget, or the next time a checkout asks for details you don’t have handy.

Proton’s offers are available to new users and include a 30-day money-back guarantee.

#10327. How AI Image-to-Image and Image-to-Video Tools Work, and How to Build a Reliable Workflow

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Creating polished visual content used to require professional equipment, editing skills and hours of manual work. Generative AI has changed that for photographers, designers, marketers and developers who build creative tools, but the results depend heavily on understanding what these systems actually do. Two techniques sit at the center of most workflows: image-to-image generation, which transforms an existing picture, and image-to-video generation, which adds motion to a still.

This article explains how both techniques work, which settings control the outcome, how several kinds of tools differ, and how to assemble a workflow with review steps, including a short code example you can run yourself. It also covers rights, authenticity and the limits of the technology. Feature details come from each vendor’s own pages at the time of writing and change often, so confirm current terms before you plan a production.

Image-to-image resembles text-to-image, but it starts from your picture instead of pure noise. According to the Hugging Face Diffusers image-to-image guide, the initial image is encoded into latent space and noise is added to it. The model then uses the prompt and the noisy latent to predict and remove that noise, and a decoder converts the result back into an image.

A single setting, usually called strength or creativity, decides how much of the original remains. In Diffusers, a higher strength gives the model more freedom, and a value of 1.0 means the starting image is largely ignored. Strength is linked to the number of denoising steps: with 50 inference steps and a strength of 0.8, the pipeline adds noise for 40 steps and then denoises for 40.

The ranges above are rules of thumb, not guarantees. Models and tools respond differently, so test on your own images. The script below, adapted from the Diffusers documentation, sweeps four strength values on one source image so you can compare them side by side.

Running this requires a compatible GPU setup and model downloads, and the checkpoint name and defaults can change between library versions, so check the current documentation.

Plain image-to-image can drift from the layout you wanted. Research such as ControlNet adds spatial control to pretrained text-to-image diffusion models, with conditions such as edges, depth, segmentation and human pose. In practice, this is why many tools offer reference images, sketches, masks and pose guides on top of a prompt: they supply structure that a text prompt cannot describe precisely.

The key advantage is speed of iteration. The main limits are consistency between variations, small details such as hands and text, and the gap between a convincing concept and a usable final asset.

The right tool depends on what you need to control and where the output will be used. The table summarizes what each vendor’s own page states, and “not stated” means the page did not say, not that the feature is absent.

OpenArt’s own page describes an AI image to image generator that accepts JPEG, PNG, WEBP or HEIC files, offers a creativity slider and an optional reference image, and lets you switch between several models. Its FAQ advises uploading only images you own or have rights to use. Adobe’s Firefly page describes generative editing and video tools and states that outputs include Content Credentials, while saying its own models were trained on licensed and public-domain content. These are vendor statements, so read the current terms for the plan you use.

Image-to-video uses a still as the starting point for an animated scene. Most current systems extend diffusion models across time, so that a whole sequence of frames is denoised together. The research paper Video Diffusion Models describes this extension from images to video. The practical consequence is that the first frame strongly shapes what follows, so a clean composition and a clear subject make better clips.

For example, a still of a person walking through a rainy city can be animated with falling rain, reflections and a slow camera move. The motion instruction should describe one clear action and one camera movement rather than a long list.

Kling’s main site announces Kling 4.0 and describes stable dynamic motion with an audio-visual experience, with image-to-video features and an API platform listed. OpenArt’s Kling 4.0 page describes how the model is offered on that platform. According to that page, videos run from 3 to 30 seconds at 720p to 1080p, you can use up to 10 keyframes and up to 15 references, and you can save a character for reuse. The page does not state pricing or generation quotas for the model. Capabilities depend on the platform that provides access and on the settings available there, so check supported inputs, output limits and cost before planning a production.

Generated motion needs checking. Warped limbs, drifting faces, flickering textures and unwanted text are common. A quick review script samples frames and reports basic file properties:

Consider a small business preparing a campaign for a new skincare product. The same steps apply to any visual project.

This process reduces the effort of exploring concepts and makes it easier to test several versions of an advertisement before choosing a direction. It does not remove the need for human review.

They can be, with different priorities. Photographers can explore creative treatments, mood boards and alternative backgrounds without replacing traditional photography. Marketers can develop campaign concepts, create variations of social graphics and test promotional videos. In both cases the value comes from supporting a clear creative objective rather than adopting a tool because it is new.

A technique that transforms an existing image using a prompt, by adding noise to the image and then regenerating it under guidance. A strength or creativity setting controls how much of the original is kept.

Use a lower strength, a reference image or a structure condition such as edges or depth where the tool supports it.

Yes. Image-to-video tools animate a still from a motion description, though complex motion can introduce inconsistencies, so review every clip.

No. Several vendors and platforms offer video generation. Compare supported inputs, length, resolution, cost and licensing against your needs.

It depends on the tool, the plan and the rights you hold in the inputs. Read the current terms and keep records of your sources.

Image-to-image and image-to-video tools give creators a faster way to explore ideas, but they work best when you understand their controls. Strength settings decide how much of the original survives, conditioning methods preserve structure, and the starting frame shapes video. Compare hosted platforms, vendor apps and open-source libraries against your needs, build review and provenance into the workflow, and treat the output as a draft that a person checks before it ships.

#10324. Tech lawsuits defined 2026 — and lawmakers want more

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Congress is scrambling to provide legal recourse for deepfakes and online grooming.

Congress is scrambling to provide legal recourse for deepfakes and online grooming.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

With just a few weeks remaining, lawmakers are trying to pass new protections for victims of online harm before the end of 2026, targeting deepfake creators and online platforms that block users from filing lawsuits.

On Tuesday, members of a Senate Judiciary subcommittee hosted a hearing calling for the end of forced arbitration for online harms. Their immediate target was Roblox, which they accused of evading legal complaints about children allegedly groomed on the platform by enforcing its own forced arbitration clause against users who agreed to the terms as minors, in some cases even under the age of 10. Separately, a few blocks away, Reps. Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez (D-NY) and Laurel Lee (R-FL) brought hotel heiress Paris Hilton to Capitol Hill to advocate for the Defiance Act, which would let victims of nonconsensual intimate deepfakes sue their creators.

“The best thing we can do in terms of enforcement is we can open up the courthouse doors,” Sen. Josh Hawley (R-MO), who chairs the Judiciary subcommittee, told reporters after the Roblox hearing. “The more I see these companies evade regulations, the more I think there is nothing as powerful as a jury.” Hawley introduced a new bill with ranking member Dick Durbin (D-IL) to render arbitration agreements unenforceable for disputes over alleged child sexual exploitation or “child cyber injury,” which could include “reputational harm, mental suffering,” or death.

“The best thing we can do in terms of enforcement is we can open up the courthouse doors”

Roblox has sought to enforce its arbitration clause in lawsuits it’s facing over allegations that users were groomed on the gaming platform as minors, despite the company’s claims of protections. Roblox is facing about 180 lawsuits in a consolidated case over its alleged role in failing to protect children. Under the arbitration process, the public would not get to see legal discovery like the kind that’s come to light under recent cases against Meta. Hawley called the jury system “corruption-proof” and “an incredibly big hammer.”

Roblox has denied that it’s trying to dodge accountability. “Safety has been central to Roblox since we launched. We require age checks for chat, place users under 16 in age-based accounts with protective defaults, and block images and video in chat,” Roblox’s global head of public policy Nicky Jackson Colaço said in a statement. “A legal position about arbitration is not a measure of a company’s safety record, and a hearing built around it fails to advance safety.”

Congress has only a little more than three more weeks left in session before the end of the year, and a new Democratic majority will be sworn in come January. Republicans are expected to do their best to push through President Donald Trump’s nominees for judgeships and executive branch posts like FCC commissioner in the lame-duck session, as well as try to pass year-end bills like the National Defense Authorization Act, which often includes a range of other policy items. But some lawmakers are holding out hope that they can open up courthouses to new litigation over deepfake abuse and child predation.

“You will be amazed by how quickly the company will change its behavior once it is sued in public court”

The Defiance Act would let victims of nonconsensual intimate deepfakes sue their creators for damages, in an attempt to raise the cost of creating these kinds of images. Hilton visited Washington earlier this year to share how an intimate non-deepfake video of her at age 19 shared without her consent impacted her life, and she used her story to advocate for the Defiance Act. At Tuesday’s press conference, she said she would come back to Congress as many times as necessary for the bill to become law. “Some people might have thought that was the end of my fight,” Hilton said. “But when I make a promise to survivors, I keep it.”

Hilton said over 50 million people have been victimized by sexually explicit deepfakes. “For me personally, strangers have created over 100,000 AI-generated explicit images and videos portraying me in the most graphic and disturbing ways imaginable,” Hilton said. “If this can happen to someone with my resources and my platform, imagine how powerless a young girl might feel facing this completely alone.”

Hilton’s comments were followed by Elliston Berry, an advocate who said a classmate made an explicit deepfake image of her friend group “from an innocent social media post” when she was 14, and distributed it throughout her school. “In a matter of seconds, my sense of security disappeared,” she said. “For me, the Defiance Act means recognition that victims deserve to be heard, that their experience matters, and that their right to seek justice should not depend on how quickly technology advances.”

#10323. One gigabyte of Wikipedia losslessly compressed to 105.39MB with self-learning AI transformer model

Reviews, benchmarks, and updates on current GPUs.

Join for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation plus sign you up to our newsletter.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

A dozen hybrid compressors can beat its score, but none relies on a neural network alone.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Tufa Labs’ tufazip has compressed 1GB of information from Wikipedia into just 105MB of space, losslessly, without using any hand-built models, according to a research post. The method uses a 163-million-parameter Transformer model as a predictor instead, and similar compression or decompression would take two Nvidia B200 GPUs about a week to manage. It’s the smallest result on the Large Text Compression Benchmark from any entry that uses a neural network as its only predictor.

We shrank 1 GB of Wikipedia to 105.39 MB, losslessly. Introducing tufazip: the best entry on the Large Text Compression Benchmark using a neural network as its sole predictor. Everything above it mixes a transformer with hand-built models.November 19, 2026

Researcher Tommy He built tufazip, and the full package, at 105.39MB after optimization, includes a small, 4.33MB pre-trained starter model — stored at low precision, or quantized, to save space — that’s needed to unpack the file. The full model is a 12-layer Transformer, which means there are 12 stages for each guess to go through. The starter stores just two already-trained stages, copying them back and forth to fill all 12 once the full model spins up in memory; each copy keeps learning on its own.

This doesn’t work like a traditional chatbot or cloud AI but is, rather, a self-contained language model that learns on the fly. Notable here is that this compression is lossless, which means there’s no risk of any data being lost in the process. This means that predictor updates must be “fully deterministic.”

Training happens in two stages. The starter, a 34-million-parameter model, is trained offline, before any compression begins. Then, the full model begins to learn online, by predicting each token and training on it, with the unpacking side repeating the same steps, with the model itself being rebuilt alongside the text. For this reason, the total size must include, besides the compressed text, only the unpacking program and the files it needs, including the starter, but not the full trained model.

There is no traditional, hand-built compression model that assists, setting it apart, Tufa Labs says, from all 12 entries that now rank above it on the benchmark, which mix a Transformer with hand-built models. Its first, online-only version ranked second when submitted in early September; the 105.39MB version was 13th as of the benchmark’s Nov. 19 update. Emphasis should be placed on this being a text-only benchmark, for those imagining media files.

While this sounds more exciting than, say, WinRAR, it’s for research purposes and not a product, and that’s underlined by what you’d need to actually run it. Currently, two Nvidia B200 GPUs — plus two Intel Xeon Platinum 8570 CPUs in the test machine — would need 631,548 seconds to compress and 645,748 seconds to decompress the 105.39MB version, or about 7.3 and 7.5 days, respectively, not counting the starter’s offline training.

The first version, which learned entirely online and came in at 105.98MB, took 647,595 and 630,136 seconds. Its GPUs peaked at 43.37 GiB of VRAM between them, and total memory use, GPUs included, peaked at roughly 68,525 MiB during compression and 73,748 MiB during decompression.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The experiment is fundamentally interesting because it shows, for example, that a smaller vocabulary can actually help: a 4K-word tokenizer beats both 8K and 16K. Giving Wikipedia’s XML markup its own dedicated tokens made the compression worse. Bigger isn’t always better, and that goes for model size, too, as a 267-million-parameter model did slightly worse than a 188-million-parameter model on this data set. With a smaller, 100MB version of the file, gains leveled off at about 77 million parameters. A run started from a bigger, six-layer offline model predicted better, with just a 99.17MB payload, but its chunkier weights made the total file larger than the online-only run.

Improving compression can be costly, as widening the window of already-processed text used for extra training, from 15 to 35 million tokens, only cut the payload 1.21% at the cost of 40% more compute. The key takeaways are that optimization isn’t always as simple as throwing more at the problem, and that trade-offs can be more expensive than expected. He frames that six-layer trade-off as the next research question: how much predictive quality a smaller model can keep, which makes the vanishing point a balance between predictive quality and model size for this benchmark.

For one or two people who want countertop reverse osmosis without a headache-inducing plumbing project, it’s an easy way to keep better-tasting water always ready to pour.

The C2SL RO System can be yours for just $133 after a 22% discount from its $179.99 list price and with 9to5Google’s promo code, 9TO5GBF26.

A Waterdrop reverse osmosis system can make sense at any of these price points. The X16 is for the household that will put its speed and controls to work. The G3P800 keeps smart features at a lower price. The G5P500A makes under-sink RO more affordable than ever. And for those with limited space, countertop models let you decide how much hot-and-cold convenience you want without ever touching your home’s plumbing system. #10318. The leaked Lenovo Legion Go Beam could be the affordable handheld we need

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

It looks comparatively cheap — and that might be a good thing in this economy.

It looks comparatively cheap — and that might be a good thing in this economy.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

For a while there, it seemed like Lenovo had given up on selling handheld gaming PCs, hiking the price of its Legion Go 2 and Legion Go S far higher than other RAM-starved competitors. A decent one can easily cost $1,000 if not $2,000 now. But it looks like Lenovo might be about to announce a relatively cheap handheld once again— the Legion Go Beam.

Windows Latest has leaked images of the handheld, and there’s no question it’s designed to be a value alternative to even the company’s least expensive Legion Go S variant. While it’s got the same big stippled grips and triggers for comfort, there’s only one USB-C port on the top instead of two, a comparatively cheap-looking D-pad, no touchpad, and only a single tiny intake and exhaust for its fan.

There’s no SteamOS logo where that would go, so it likely runs Windows instead… but then there’s that name, “Beam.” Perhaps you’ll “beam” your games to it because it’s a streaming and cloud gaming handheld, instead of one that primarily plays games on its own chip? Maybe that’s why it doesn’t need as much airflow as previous handhelds?

Perhaps there’s a partnership with Xbox Cloud Gaming specifically, and that’s why it’s all lit up in Xbox green?

But it might just be green because it looks nice with white, which has been the “value” color for handhelds so far (see: ROG Ally, Xbox Ally, and the Windows-based Legion Go S). And it might be “Beam” because there’s a literal beam of light on the bottom, even though the first thing I do with most handhelds is turn any and all distracting RGB lighting off. Or maybe it’s “Beam” just because it sounds like a friendly, fun, value-oriented name, instead of something “S” or “Pro.”

I won’t begin to speculate on the Legion Go Beam’s price, except to say that “cheap” may be relative. The Legion Go S was originally supposed to cost $499, but wound up costing far more except on the occasional sale. The Legion Go 2 was supposed to start at $1,099 but wound up costing $1,500 and up after RAMageddon. Streaming-first handhelds have been far cheaper, though, like the $249 Sony PlayStation Portal.

Lenovo has a history of announcing its new gaming devices at CES in January each year, including previous Legion Go handhelds, so I’m expecting to see an official announcement of the Lenovo Legion Go Beam then. Even if so, that doesn’t mean it’ll be available in January — there’s often a many-month lag.

#10317. At $600, the Pixel 10 is this year’s no-brainer Black Friday smartphone deal

Phones have gotten a lot more expensive over the last year or so, and it’s a trend we’re expecting to get worse before it can get better. If you’re in the market for an upgrade, this year’s Black Friday might be one of the final truly good times to buy a new device, and for just $600, last year’s Pixel 10 should be high on your list.

While buying year-old hardware typically comes with some pretty deep sacrifices between generations, that’s not particularly true with the Pixel 10 this year. From a hardware perspective, nearly everything is either identical or close enough to not particularly matter. Its design and display are all but unchanged, Qi2-ready magnets come on both models, and both devices are built with the same level of battery longevity in mind. And much to the chagrin of many a Pixel die-hard, Tensor G5 and Tensor G6 are practically interchangeable.

So what are you giving up that matters? When the Pixel 11 arrived, software features made up the bulk of this list, but with last week’s Pixel Drop, a lot of the gaps between these two generations have been closed. Gemini Intelligence with Rambler, app automation, and more are in the process of rolling out to existing Pixel 10 users, and new features like Create My Widget arrived on the Pixel 10 and 11 at the exact same time. From a software perspective — Google’s bread and butter, in my eyes — the only big sacrifice comes from the year of software support that’s already complete, leaving you with just six instead of seven.

Realistically, the camera is the one place you’re likely to feel a difference. The Pixel 10 was the first non-Pro device in Google’s Tensor era to ship with a telephoto lens, but it came with some big sacrifices. The main sensor is effectively an A-series lens, and taken together, the entire package might leave something to be desired. It’s far from a bad camera though — I used it as my main shooter through most of its lifecycle — and while the Pixel 11 did get some upgrades here, I think real shutterbugs would be better off looking at Google’s Pro lineup anyway. You’ll also lose out on things like HiLight, though really, are you missing that much?

What makes this deal so good is really its place in the market, not as a direct comparison to the newer Pixel 11. For $600, Google’s competition is going to have a hard time competing with the value the Pixel 10 delivers. Hell, even Google might find it difficult to compete in the coming months; at full price, $600 is what the Pixel 10a goes for now, a decidedly less-impressive device compared to this model. No, this is a flagship smartphone through and through, and for $600, you’re effectively guaranteeing a reliable, powerful smartphone experience from now until 2032 — hopefully enough time for this whole RAM crisis to finally simmer down a bit.

#10314. Grand Theft Auto VI understands just how important a smartphone is

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Text messages, social networking, selfies — the device is integrated into every aspect of your virtual life.

Text messages, social networking, selfies — the device is integrated into every aspect of your virtual life.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Grand Theft Auto VI, in many ways, tries to simulate real life. And life in 2026 would not be complete without a smartphone. GTA VI understands this and makes the phone not just a central part of the experience, but an object that exists within the world. You may try to escape into a fantasy of robbing banks and fighting drug lords, but you can’t get away from texts from your mom or nagging fitness app notifications.

Seeing a smartphone in a game isn’t a new phenomenon. The device has become a natural stand-in for the main menu in many games, giving players an interface that feels intuitive, because a phone is something they use every day. But the phone in GTA VI is different for a few reasons. One is that it behaves much like any other object in the game, whether it’s a gun or a bottle of beer. The action doesn’t pause when you use your phone. Instead, the game switches to a first-person viewpoint, where you can still walk around — or drive, if you’re feeling ambitious — while reading texts or flipping through a fictional version of Instagram.

Rob Nelson, the co-studio head at developer Rockstar North and producer on GTA VI, says that it would’ve felt “archaic” to have a static phone that worked like those in other games, or the one in GTA V, which appeared as a pop-up at the bottom of the screen across from the minimap. “We felt that it was important to make it physical so you have to look at it and put your attention on it, including while driving,” he explains. “It’s about finding the line between realism and player convenience. But the act of being lost in your phone felt like something worth doing, even though it could mean slightly more inconvenience. In this game you are committing to using it.” (Of course, texting and driving, like much of what you do in GTA VI, is both very dangerous and very illegal.)

You have to interact with the phone in the game constantly. Often the story plays out through phone calls and text messages, and you have a surprisingly large array of apps to use that serve a real purpose in the game. You manage your money in a banking app, use a rideshare app when you need a lift, track your workouts in a fitness app, scroll through a social network complete with comments and the ability to like posts, and sideload an AI tool that scans nearby cars so you can see how easy they’ll be to steal. You can even change the wallpaper and ringtone. (GTA VI has two main characters that you swap between, and each has their own phone, though they’re functionally identical.)

What’s most impressive is how all of these apps interact with each other. For instance, after pulling off a major heist, a friend might text you a news story about it, asking “was this you!?” You can then click the link, open up the news app, and read an actual article about what went down. These kinds of interactions can be functional, like when another character texts you a location, which you can then add to your map so the GPS can guide you there. Other times, they add texture to the world or depth to the characters. GTA VI is a love story about two criminals named Jason and Lucia, and you play as both. At one point, while controlling Lucia, I got a text asking for a selfie. I opened up the camera, shot a pic of Lucia drinking a latte, and sent it over. Jason replied immediately — and their relationship got a boost.

Phones are so fully integrated into the game that they can even interrupt the characters’ sleep. At the end of each day, you can click on a bed to rest for the night. But neither Jason nor Lucia will instantly fall asleep when you do — instead, they’ll sit down and pull out their phone, and you have the option to doomscroll or catch up on texts instead of going to sleep right away. Nelson says that this was implemented because the developers wanted to include the ability for the characters to sit on the bed at night, much like a real couple in a shared living space might do. “We needed something to do aside from just sitting there staring at nothing,” he says.

If nothing else, GTA VI’s phone shows how alike we all are: Even bank robbers being hunted by the police can’t seem to stop scrolling. “Phones are a constant part of our lives,” Nelson says of the evolution, “even more than they were in 2013.”

#10312. Honor just supercharged its smartwatch lineup with a new Pro model

The Honor Watch 6 Pro has a 5,500-nit OLED display, ceramic bezel, 18-day battery life, and costs less than it looks.

In the world of athletic smartwatches, it isn’t often that you could mistake one for a luxury watch, but Honor seems to want to flip all that on its head with the Honor Watch 6 Pro. Just take a look at this excerpt from the company’s press release:

Framing this brilliant display is a Precision Nanocrystalline Ceramic bezel crafted through a 1400°C high-temperature integrated sintering process, delivering a delicate texture and rich visual depth.

Inspired by modernist architecture, its diamond-cut octagonal rotatable crown features a dual-layer horizontal and vertical cut, complemented by a side button for intuitive navigation.

That sounds pretty wild to me, and it’s backed up by specs that sound just as legit, too. On top is a 1.5-inch LTPO Tandem AMOLED display that outputs 5,500-nit peak brightness. Underneath is a bevy of new sensors called the Intelligent Sense System, which Honor says combines “multi-wavelength monitoring, multi-optical path patrol technology, and anti-interference glass technology to reach deep skin layers.”

Runners will be pleased to know that the Honor Watch 6 Pro not only incorporates a dual-band GPS, but combines it with a “star-pointing 3-antenna design” and an AI fusion algorithm, which should deliver ultra-accurate tracking in any location and no matter how you hold your wrist. Despite its luxury looks, this one tracks over 120 sports modes, including Honor’s Professional Running Coach feature, which tracks running posture, lactate threshold, heart rate targets, and keeps you on pace with real-time voice coaching.

During sports modes like Badminton, the watch can identify more than six types of swings, and Honor says other sports modes can deliver similarly detailed statistics. The watch is 5ATM, IP68, IP69, and IP69K water-and-dust-resistant rated, plus it integrates directly with Strava so you can pull all your fitness data together.

Honor is incorporating a new wrist twist gesture system this time around, letting you use gestures to perform actions hands-free. The watch is rated at 10 days of battery life on a single charge in regular usage mode, while endurance mode will stretch that to 18 days. Pre-sales begin today, November 19, and run until December 31, when the watch is expected to launch, starting at €279/£229 for early bird pricing. After launch, the retail MSRP starts at €379/£329.

#10311. Markdown to PDF in 2026: No-Install Methods and the Rendering Problems Nobody Warns You About

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Converting Markdown to PDF is easy until the document has real content: long code blocks, wide tables, images and non-Latin text are where most tools fall apart.

Pandoc with LaTeX is still the most powerful route, but it needs a large install and trips over Unicode and emoji unless you switch engines and fonts.

Browser-based approaches (print CSS, Chromium-powered converters, in-browser tools) avoid the install and give you HTML-quality rendering, as long as you control page breaks.

A handful of print CSS rules (@page, break-inside: avoid, break-after: avoid) fix the majority of ugly PDFs, whichever tool you use.

Choose the tool by the document: a quick README export, a long technical report and a multilingual document each have a different best option.

I write almost everything in Markdown: READMEs, release notes, client handovers, meeting notes. And at least once a week, someone asks for "the PDF version". Every time, the conversion looks fine on the first page and falls apart somewhere around page three: a code block split in half, a table running off the edge, an emoji replaced by an empty box.

SitePoint covered the classic route back in 2015 with Creating PDFs from Markdown with Pandoc and LaTeX. That approach still works. But a lot has changed since then, and today there are several ways to get a clean PDF without installing a TeX distribution. This article compares them, then goes through the rendering problems you'll actually hit, with a fix for each.

Pandoc is still the Swiss Army knife. The simplest command is:

By default, that goes through LaTeX, which means you need a TeX distribution installed (several gigabytes for a full one). The output is beautiful for prose and math, but LaTeX has strong opinions about layout, and customizing it means writing LaTeX.

Pandoc can also render through an HTML engine instead, which lets you style the PDF with CSS:

Best for: long documents, academic writing, math, automated pipelines.

Watch out for: install size, Unicode handling with the default engine (more on that below).

Render the Markdown to HTML (any previewer, a static site, a GitHub page) and print it to PDF. It's free and already installed everywhere, but you inherit whatever the page looks like on screen: navigation bars, sidebars, and page breaks wherever they happen to fall.

Best for: quick, one-off exports of short documents.

Watch out for: zero control over page breaks unless the page ships print CSS.

Extensions like Markdown PDF for VS Code, or CLI tools like md-to-pdf, render your Markdown to HTML and print it with a headless Chromium:

You get the same rendering quality as a browser, plus a config file for CSS, margins, headers and footers. The trade-off is a Node install and a downloaded Chromium.

Best for: developers who live in their editor or want CI-friendly exports.

Watch out for: the first run downloads a browser, and print CSS is still your job.

The newest option is tools that do the whole conversion inside the browser tab: you paste or drop the .md file, preview the actual pages and download the PDF. The good ones don't upload your file anywhere, which matters for client documents and internal notes.

Best for: people who don't want to install anything, non-developers on your team, private documents.

Watch out for: many online converters do upload your file to a server; check before you paste anything confidential.

Whichever method you pick, the same handful of problems show up as soon as the document is longer than a page. Most of them are fixed with print CSS, which works in Pandoc's HTML engines, Chromium-based tools and any HTML you print from a browser.

A 20-line function cut after line 3, with the rest on the next page, is the most common complaint. The fix is to ask the renderer not to break inside the block:

One caveat: a block longer than a full page has to break. For those, it's better to let it break cleanly than to push it to a new page and leave a huge gap.

On screen, a long line in a code block scrolls horizontally. On paper, there's no scrollbar, so it's simply cut off. Wrap long lines in print:

Tables with many columns overflow the page or get squeezed until every cell is one word per line. Three things help: a smaller font size for tables, a landscape page for the worst offenders, and avoiding breaks in the middle of short tables:

If a table genuinely needs eight columns, consider whether it should be a table in the PDF at all, or an appendix in landscape.

A heading alone at the bottom of a page, with its content on the next one, looks broken. This rule keeps a heading attached to what follows it:

Support for break-after: avoid varies between rendering engines, so test it with yours; when it isn't honored, a manual page break before the heading is the reliable fallback.

Markdown has no syntax for a page break. The usual trick is a raw HTML element that print CSS turns into a break:

Some converters also recognize a comment like , which has the advantage of staying invisible when the same file is rendered on GitHub.

Images are inserted at their natural size, so a 4000-pixel screenshot spills off the page. Cap them:

For blurry images, the problem is usually the opposite: a small screenshot stretched to page width. Export screenshots at 2x if they're going into a PDF.

This one is specific to the LaTeX route. With the default pdflatex engine, Unicode characters outside its supported set produce errors or simply vanish. Switch to XeLaTeX or LuaLaTeX and give it a font that actually contains the characters:

Emoji are harder still in LaTeX and often need a dedicated package. HTML-based renderers handle both far better, because they use the operating system's font fallback just like a web page does.

Internal links ([see setup](#setup)) and external links should stay clickable. HTML-based renderers usually keep both. If yours doesn't, check that headings get IDs (most Markdown parsers add them) and that the PDF engine preserves link annotations.

Put together, these rules make a solid starting point for any HTML-based route:

Save it as print.css and pass it to Pandoc (--css=print.css with an HTML engine), to md-to-pdf through its config, or include it in the HTML you print from the browser.

My rule of thumb after a lot of trial and error:

A quick README or notes export, no install, private content: an in-browser converter. Full disclosure: this need is why I built the free Markdown to PDF converter on AwesomeToolkit, and it's what I use for this case now. It renders real A4 or Letter pages as you type, has an option to keep code blocks and tables whole, adds page numbers and a table of contents, and the conversion happens in the browser, so the file isn't uploaded anywhere.

Documentation in a repo, exported in CI: md-to-pdf or Pandoc with an HTML engine, plus the stylesheet above.

Long reports, math, academic formatting: Pandoc with XeLaTeX, using a font that covers every script in the document.

Something you'll print once and forget: the browser's Print to PDF is fine.

Whatever you choose, the biggest quality jump comes from the print rules, not the tool. Ten lines of CSS turn "it looks fine until page three" into a PDF you can actually send.

Yes. Your browser's Print to PDF works on any rendered Markdown, and in-browser converters do the whole conversion in a tab. Check that the converter processes files locally if the content is private.

The default pdflatex engine has limited Unicode support. Use --pdf-engine=xelatex or lualatex and set a font that contains the characters with -V mainfont= (and -V CJKmainfont= for CJK text).

Markdown has no native syntax for it. Insert

where you want the break, or use the page-break comment your converter supports.

Add pre { break-inside: avoid; } to your print CSS. Blocks longer than a page will still break, which is expected.

With HTML-based renderers, usually yes, both external links and links to headings inside the document. Test internal links in particular, since they depend on headings having IDs.
