#10309. The wait begins for GTA VI on PC

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Given how good the game is on console, the wait will almost certainly be worth it.

Given how good the game is on console, the wait will almost certainly be worth it.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Grand Theft Auto VI is finally here for millions of players who own a PlayStation 5 or Xbox Series X / S, and those who plan to buy a console, despite rising costs this year, just to play it. As you have likely already noticed, the launch is inescapable, and that’s especially tough if you’re a PC gamer.

I’m sorry to remind you that the clock has only just now started ticking down to the unannounced yet inevitable release of the PC version. Good luck with avoiding Twitch and browsing the internet while trying to remain oblivious to the violent (and romantic) delights that await you.

Seeing Leonida in true 4K at an unlocked frame rate with full ray-tracing effects and extended draw distances, among other graphical improvements, might be a thing you can do on your PC as early as sometime in Q1 2027, if recent reports are accurate. Regardless, early 2027 is not right now. Speaking as someone with a capable gaming desktop, I’m sad to say that FOMO has already gotten the best of me. I bought GTA VI on the PS5 and will probably double-dip and buy it again on PC, just like I did with GTA V and Red Dead Redemption 2. The psychology of me, a weak-willed consumer, is one that publisher Rockstar Games and really any other company in its position would be happy to exploit.

The PC version of GTA VI will launch, eventually, in a very different landscape than the PC ports of Red Dead Redemption 2 and GTA V did. Despite the recent downturn in PC sales driven by increased RAM prices, there are considerably more players on PC now than there were a decade ago. SteamDB’s charts show that Steam’s concurrent users alone have more than doubled (from around 20 million to over 40 million now) since 2020.

A chunk of those people are likely made up of console expats who decided to migrate to PC for various reasons. Some wanted to experience the fun of building their own desktop, while some were mainly convinced by the fact that you can get more power for your money with a PC than with a PlayStation 5 Pro or Xbox Series X. For a subset of people, the convenience of getting something like a Steam Deck to bring your PC game library with you anywhere more than makes up for its lack of power. There are many other justifiable reasons for making PC your main gaming platform, like getting faster access to discounts on digital games, not paying for online multiplayer, ultrawide monitor resolutions, being able to buy games from one of several platforms, and having one device that you can use for work and play.

Rockstar seems poised to deliver the definitive version of GTA VI on PC on its own terms, and I’m interested to see what the end result will be. In addition to building the best-looking version of the game made to run well across countless configurations, its PC optimization efforts are likely also more focused than before on ensuring decent performance on lower-end hardware (including PC handhelds). With a 200-plus-gigabyte install size on consoles, I dread how big it’ll be on PC.

Ideally, the PC version will launch alongside an improved version of GTA Online (whether it’s free to play is anyone’s guess), and maybe with upgraded GTA roleplay tech to support it. I’m hopeful that the PC version of GTA VI will come in early 2027, but if the numerous delays of the console versions were any indication, the developer will take all the time it needs to get it right.

Update, 9:55AM ET, November 19th: The SteamDB data cited now specifies that it’s concurrent users, not the number of users total.

#10300. What Is the Best UI design Tool? 8 Tools Compared by the Job They Do (2026)

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

The ideal UI testing tool is the one that discovers what your users discover first: broken layouts, usability issues that no longer work, and new accessibility issues in all the browsers your users use before a change goes into the repository. In 2026, most frontend teams don't rely on a single tool to do all of that. The best setups involve two layers: a test runner layer that launches the browser and monitors behavior, and a UI testing and review platform that visually checks the outcome and routes changes to the right stakeholders for sign-off.

Even with the right stack, it still relies on what your team needs to catch. Different teams will focus on different points: a QA team trying to automate checkout flows, a design system team defending shared components, and a single developer working on a tight budget will all be somewhere different. This guide is categorized by the job these UI testing tools do, then connects them.

Most "best UI testing tool" lists rank test runners and visual testing platforms against each other as if they were interchangeable. They aren't. UI testing covers four distinct jobs:

Functional testing: Does the UI respond correctly when a user clicks, types, or hovers?

Visual testing: Does the UI still look right across layout, color, typography, and spacing?

Accessibility testing: Can people using keyboards and screen readers use it, and did this change introduce new violations?

Cross-browser and device coverage: Does it work in Safari on mobile, not just Chrome on a developer's laptop?

There are also other levels of UI tests. Unit tests test the logic but don't interact with the UI. End-to-end (E2E) tests represent the full user journey in a browser but are difficult to set up, hard to troubleshoot when the bug is a visual detail, and can't practically test every state. Between the extremes of unit tests and E2E tests, component tests simulate each component's behavior in a real browser using fixed inputs, offering speed closer to unit tests and user-level coverage closer to E2E. Many teams maintain a thin layer of E2E tests for critical journeys, and they have component tests for the dozens of states a component may be in.

These tools are organized by job so you can stack them rather than choose one winner

These platforms sit on top of your test runner. They capture rendered UI, compare it to approved baselines, and give your team a place to review what changed.

Chromatic is a UI testing and review platform made by the Storybook team. It catches visual, interaction, and accessibility bugs in components and pages before they merge, and it gives developers, designers, and product managers one place to review UI changes and sign off. Chromatic's team is the core maintainer of Storybook, which has about 90,000 GitHub stars and 17 million weekly npm downloads, and a Vitest core maintainer on the team built Chromatic's Vitest integration. Chromatic is used by 45 Fortune 100 companies, including GitHub, Shopify, Microsoft, Adobe, the BBC, and monday.com.

Pricing: Free plan with 5,000 snapshots per month (Chrome only), unlimited projects and users. Starter is $179/month for 35,000 snapshots and adds Safari, Firefox, and Edge. Pro is $399/month for 85,000 snapshots. Enterprise is custom, with SSO, SCIM, SLA support, and data retention. TurboSnap snapshots count as 0.2 of a regular snapshot.

Trade-offs: Chromatic is strongest when your UI is built from components. Teams with no component tests can start from Playwright or Cypress E2E tests, but they'll get the most value once components are covered too. Snapshot usage also grows with each browser and viewport you add, so plan coverage before you scale it.

Best for: Design system teams, product teams that want designers and PMs to approve UI changes, React Native teams, and Vitest-first teams that want visual testing without baselines stored in the repo.

Percy is BrowserStack's visual testing platform. It captures screenshots from your existing tests, renders them across browsers and responsive widths, and highlights pixel differences for review.

Strengths: Framework-agnostic SDKs for Playwright, Cypress, Selenium, and Storybook. App Percy covers native mobile apps. The free plan includes 5,000 screenshots per month with unlimited users and projects.

Trade-offs: Percy is page-oriented, and each page rendered in each browser at each width counts as a separate screenshot, so usage climbs quickly with cross-browser coverage. Percy compares feature branches against a base branch, which teams with complex branching should test early. Reviewers see rendered screenshots rather than an inspectable copy of the code under test, so debugging a diff usually means reproducing it locally.

Pricing: Free tier, then paid plans with fixed screenshot allowances and overage.

Best for: Teams already paying for BrowserStack that want visual checks on page-level E2E tests.

Applitools Eyes uses Visual AI to compare screenshots the way a person would, with match levels that can ignore content changes and focus on layout. Its Ultrafast Grid re-renders captured page states across many browser, viewport, and device combinations.

Strengths: Mature AI matching, broad browser and device coverage, Figma and Storybook testing, accessibility testing, and an MCP server. Higher tiers add AI-driven functional testing and plain-English test authoring.

Trade-offs: Applitools is priced and packaged for enterprises. There's a free trial but no free plan, and the Starter plan is $667/month billed annually for 100,000 component checkpoints or 1,000 page checkpoints. Professional and Enterprise are custom-priced.

Best for: Large QA organizations with big cross-browser matrices and budget for a dedicated visual AI platform.

BackstopJS is a free, open-source visual regression testing tool released under the MIT license. You list the URLs, viewports, and CSS selectors to capture in a config file, and BackstopJS renders them in a headless browser through Playwright or Puppeteer, then compares the results against approved reference screenshots. The core workflow runs on three commands: backstop init, backstop test, and backstop approve.

Strengths: Completely free, with no screenshot limits. A built-in browser report shows reference and test images side by side with a visual diff inspector, and changes can be approved from there. Optional Docker rendering keeps screenshots consistent between laptops and CI; Playwright adds Firefox and WebKit, and click, hover, and keypress selectors capture interactive states. JUnit reports plug into existing CI pipelines.

Trade-offs: BackstopJS is self-hosted, so there's no cloud dashboard, shared team review, or pull request status checks out of the box. Reference screenshots are usually committed to your repository, and every page or component state you want to test needs its own URL and scenario in the config. Someone on the team also has to own the setup, CI integration, and maintenance.

Best for: Developers and small teams with no budget who are comfortable running and maintaining their own visual regression setup.

Test runners click, type, and assert. They're free and open source, and every platform above builds on them. On their own, they offer limited visual testing: built-in screenshot assertions store baselines in your repository, rendering can drift between laptops and CI, and designers have no review interface.

Playwright is Microsoft's open-source browser automation framework. It runs tests across Chromium, Firefox, and WebKit, with auto-waiting, trace viewer debugging and code generation, and supports JavaScript, TypeScript, Python, Java, and .NET.

Visual testing: Playwright's toHaveScreenshot assertion compares screenshots against baselines stored in Git. It works, but baselines must be regenerated per operating system, and accepting a change means rerunning with an update flag.

Best for: Teams automating critical user journeys such as sign-up, checkout and login. Pair it with a platform like Chromatic for visual review across real browsers.

Cypress is an open-source testing tool that runs inside the browser, with time-travel debugging that shows the app at each step of a test. It supports both E2E and component testing.

Visual testing: Cypress has no built-in visual comparison and relies on plugins or a platform such as Chromatic, Percy or Applitools. Cypress Cloud, its paid service, adds recording, parallelization, flake detection and add-ons for accessibility and UI coverage.

Best for: Frontend developers who want fast feedback and an interactive test runner while they build.

Storybook is the open source workshop for building UI components in isolation. Each story captures one component state, and those stories double as test cases.

Testing features: Interaction tests with play functions and Testing Library, accessibility checks with the a11y addon, and a Vitest integration that runs stories as component tests.

Visual testing: Storybook doesn't diff screenshots on its own. Chromatic, built by the same team, is its visual testing and review layer.

Best for: Design system and component library teams, and any team practicing component-driven development.

Vitest Browser Mode runs component tests in a real browser instead of a simulated DOM, using Playwright or WebdriverIO under the hood.

Visual testing: Vitest's toMatchScreenshot assertion stores screenshots in the repository. For large projects, this can bloat the repo and baselines drift between machines. Chromatic can take Vitest Browser Mode tests with no code changes and run them in a stable cloud environment instead.

Best for: Teams already writing Vitest tests who want them to check how the UI looks without adopting a new framework.

Start from the problem your team is trying to fix:

"Shared component changes keep breaking other teams' screens." Test at the component level with Storybook or Vitest Browser Mode and Chromatic, so the change shows up on the pull request that caused it.

"Our Playwright tests pass, but the layout still breaks in Safari." Functional assertions don't check appearance. Add a visual testing platform that renders in Safari, such as Chromatic, Percy or Applitools.

"We turned off screenshot tests because of false alarms." Look for deterministic component states and flake controls such as Chromatic's SteadySnap, or AI matching such as Applitools.

"Designers find UI bugs after release." Choose a platform with a review and approval workflow, and make sign-off a required pull request check.

"AI coding agents keep inventing components." Give agents access to your real component library, for example through Chromatic's hosted MCP, and run every agent-built change through the same UI tests as human code.

"We have no budget." Use Playwright or Vitest screenshot assertions, self-host BackstopJS, or start on the free tiers of Chromatic or Percy.

A practical, well-covered setup for a React web app looks like this:

Unit tests in Vitest for logic and utilities.

Storybook stories or Vitest Browser Mode tests for every meaningful component state, with interaction tests on key components.

Chromatic running visual, interaction and accessibility tests on those components in Chrome, Firefox, Safari and Edge on every pull request, with designers assigned as reviewers.

A thin layer of Playwright E2E tests for sign-up, checkout and other critical journeys, also captured in Chromatic so page-level visual changes go through the same review.

This keeps most UI coverage in fast, deterministic component tests, while E2E tests confirm the journeys that matter most still work end to end.

Playwright is the best free tool for E2E UI testing, and Storybook is the best free tool for testing component states. For visual testing, For visual testing, Chromatic and Percy both offer free tiers of around 5,000 snapshots or screenshots per month, and BackstopJS is free to self-host.

UI testing is the broad practice of checking that an interface works for users, including functional, visual and accessibility checks. Visual testing is one part of it: comparing rendered UI against approved baselines to catch unintended changes in layout, color, typography and spacing.

No. Chromatic was built by the Storybook team, but it also runs visual tests from Vitest Browser Mode, Playwright and Cypress tests, and from React Native Storybook on iOS and Android simulators.

Playwright's toHaveScreenshot covers basic visual checks, but baselines live in your repo, rendering can differ between machines, and there's no review interface for designers. Teams with several reviewers or many browsers usually add a platform on top.

Most teams use both. Component tests cover individual UI states quickly and reliably, while a smaller set of E2E tests confirms that critical user journeys work across the whole app.

Get the freshest news and resources for developers, designers and digital creators in your inbox each week

#10299. Homelabber’s second-gen Optane build uses two RTX 3090s to run 2.8-trillion-parameter Kimi AI model at 3.11 tokens per second

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Faster now, but the builder says the Optane sticks still need fans of their own.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A homelabber detailed improvements to the second-gen Optane AI build in a follow-up post on Reddit, showing the addition of two RTX 3090s to run the 2.8-trillion-parameter Moonshot Kimi K3 model at 3.11 tokens per second. Transferring almost half the model onto a RAM drive added a significant 41% to generation speed and 26% to prompt evaluation. The extra fans are still needed, though, as the persistent Optane memory gets very hot.

That speed is not the most impressive, but it comes with 16.8 tokens per second for prompt evaluation, with 45 of 94 shards, or 414 GB out of 865 GB, living on a RAM drive. Such an arrangement is not new to enthusiasts who would set aside system memory for extra-fast performance in a pseudo-non-volatile manner — it’s still volatile, but appears like a disk — although not everyone has that much RAM sitting around free these days. With both GPUs but no RAM drive, the build ran at 2.20 tokens per second for generation and 13.3 for prompt evaluation.

45 of 94 shards (414 GB of 865 GB) in a tmpfs RAM drive, the rest in Optane

Builder’s measurements; llama.cpp, DAX on for the Optane portion.

Even a quick read of the details makes it clear that the GPUs aren’t holding the model: two RTX 3090s, at 24 GB apiece, only have a total of 48 GB of VRAM, which is about 5.5% of the 865 GB model file. That said, the model is a mixture-of-experts (MoE) type, where its weights are split into multiple “experts” with only a few run per token. Any given token will have “routed” experts selected for it, while “shared” experts run on every token.

For this model, with 896 experts, 16 are chosen per token, with two shared, with an active 104B parameters out of its 2.8T. All 2.8T parameters would take about 5.6 TB at 16-bit precision. Moonshot’s own release, which uses the 4-bit MXFP4 format, comes to about 1.56 TB, and the Q2_K file used here shrinks that to 865 GB, an average of under 3 bits per parameter. The 865 GB figure is likely in gibibytes (GiB), but for clarity we’re using the GB values as written by the homelabber.

The configuration here has the routed-expert tensors stay on the CPU side, originally in Optane, and now in Optane plus the RAM drive. The RTX 3090s handle attention, or how each token weighs the rest of the context, and the dense feed-forward network layer that isn’t split into experts, where every token passes through it. They also manage the shared experts, other active-dense components, the KV cache, and the stored context of the current conversation, with the GPU compute buffers. Basically, the parts on the GPUs are “used on every token,” and the improvements there come from the high level of GPU compute and memory bandwidth.

DRAM is a lot slower than VRAM, but it’s still capable of giving a decent speedup. The new build involves using Linux’s tmpfs, which, per the kernel documentation, “is a file system which keeps all of its files in virtual memory.” Those shards now live in memory, which admittedly is not ideal when the original build was designed to save money by using Optane instead. With those shards in DRAM, which the builder has said is faster than Optane, that part of the model no longer has to be read from the slower memory; the workload is decidedly distributed.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

The speed that came with the GPUs is within the range of what the builder expected, guessing “somewhere between 2 and 4 tokens per second” in a prediction from the original build thread. The GPU install had to wait, in part, for modifications to the case, which required cutting out some of the metal and relocating the PSU to make room for the GPUs and their cooling. Cooling remains a huge issue, with “the improved throughput [not changing] the original thermal warning,” and the two 12W blower fans for the Optane DIMMs are kept in place. Add in dual 350W-class GPUs, and the changes are understandable.

Things won’t stop here, as more optimizations are possible, including “customized quantization,” which may be similar to the techniques used by lawyer Nick Mermiges. Quantization selected per tensor or layer, or by where the data lives, might reduce bandwidth and capacity pressures for the slower tiers; the setup “might be faster yet.” Speed, or tokens per second alone, is not enough, and output quality must be taken into consideration in any future plan.

Valve announced some updates about Steam Machine availability today, including good news if you want to buy one right now and potential bad news about future supply due to RAMaggedon.

The good news: You can now purchase a 512GB Steam Machine without getting on a waitlist. Initially, interested buyers had to get on Valve’s waitlist if they wanted to purchase one, but the console-like PC is now available in an “add-to-cart” status, according to the company. As I write this, the 512GB versions with and without a bundled controller are showing estimated delivery dates of three to five business days on Valve’s website. If Valve sells through its current inventory, the company says it will indicate that the product is out of stock instead of making another waitlist.

The bad news: If you’re on the waitlist for a 2TB model, you might not get a chance to buy one at all, as Valve says there are more people on the waitlist than there are units available. The company is closing the waitlists for its 2TB models.

Valve says that while it’s still building more Steam machines, “memory and storage costs continue to increase at unprecedented levels” and that it doesn’t know the number it can make or “at what price we’ll be able to offer them.” The 2TB Steam Machine is “especially constrained.” The company anticipates “having more information” to share about additional supply in January.

After delaying the Steam Machine’s expected early 2026 launch due to the memory and storage crisis, Valve launched the product in June with a price starting at $1,049 for the 512GB model without a bundled controller. A 2TB model without a controller costs $1,349.

#10287. Google’s 3D emoji has wild Android spec requirements: 10GB of free space, 6GB RAM

Google introduced 3D emoji on Pixel this year, and has just detailed the minimum specifications required to get them on your Android phone.

Update: Google replied on Reddit to “clear up some confusion” about the Help Center page. The listed storage and RAM requirements refer to the “automatic background download via Google Play services on eligible Android 17 devices that didn’t ship with the 3D font preinstalled.” The support article did not make that distinction.

Noto 3D emoji, with its high-resolution bitmap approach, uses less than 220 MB of storage versus the previous 2D vector font that comes in at around 3 MB.

During installation, Google Play services temporarily needs a bit of extra headroom to copy the file into place before immediately clearing its download cache.

Meanwhile, Google said RAM usage is “minimal & transient” as “Android doesn’t load the 3D emoji font file into RAM.” The support page listed 6 GB as a “cutoff to ensure lower-memory devices stay on the lightweight ~3 MB 2D vector font.”

Noto 3D emoji goes from vector-based 2D emoji to a high-resolution bitmap font. This results in “high-resolution designs” for emoji “with more texture and dimension to characters and icons.” Google has an FAQ section about why the new emoji might look low resolution:

Vector vs. bitmap rendering: Older Android emoji used 2D vector graphics, which scale infinitely to any size without losing resolution. The 3D emoji are bitmap graphics, which are pre-rendered pixel-based images. When these images are scaled up by an app or zoomed in, the pixels stretch, which can cause a softer or lower-resolution appearance.

The Google support article published this week lists the minimum specs your Android device must have to get 3D emoji:

The last two bullet points are rather surprising. In fact, Google even lists instructions for checking how much memory and storage your device has:

In terms of availability, Google says a supported device will “automatically download the 3D emoji when it’s on Wi-Fi and charging.”

At this time, there isn’t a way to force download the emoji.

Google announced 3D emoji in May, with the new design shipping on the Pixel 11 in August. It has since arrived on older Pixel devices.

In terms of OEM availability, Google explicitly says “Samsung devices use a proprietary emoji font and do not receive the 3D font update.” Additionally:

This is also true for third-party messaging apps, such as WhatsApp, who use their own emoji fonts rather than relying on your device’s system font.

#10286. Build Live Video Chat With WebRTC: From First Call to Production Debugging

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Adding real-time video to a web app used to mean plugins, proprietary SDKs and a lot of money. Today every modern browser ships with WebRTC, a set of APIs that lets two browsers exchange audio, video and data directly. The catch is that WebRTC gives you building blocks, not a finished feature. This guide walks through those blocks in the order you actually need them, with the exact API calls you need, and then covers the production issues that tutorials usually skip.

A browser-to-browser call has four moving parts:

Start with the local preview. Browsers only allow camera access on HTTPS or localhost, so serve your page accordingly.

1.     Request a stream with navigator.mediaDevices.getUserMedia(), passing video constraints (an ideal width of 1280 and height of 720 is a sensible default) and audio constraints with echoCancellation and noiseSuppression enabled.

2.     Assign the returned MediaStream to the srcObject property of your local video element.

3.     Keep a reference to the stream, because you will add its tracks to the peer connection later.

Set the muted and playsinline attributes on the local video element. Without muted users hear themselves, and without playsinline iOS Safari opens the video full screen.

The peers need a way to find each other. A tiny Node.js server using the ws package is enough for development. Its whole job is to relay messages between the peers in a room:

1.     Create a WebSocket server (for example with the ws package) listening on its own port.

2.     Keep a map of room names to the set of sockets in each room.

3.     When a client sends a join message, store the room name on the socket and add it to that room's set.

4.     For every other message type, forward the raw message to the other sockets in the same room, and never echo it back to the sender.

5.     On disconnect, remove the socket from its room so stale peers do not receive messages.

In production you would add authentication, limit room size to two for one-to-one calls, and validate message types, but the shape stays the same.

Both sides create an RTCPeerConnection, add their local tracks and listen for the remote ones:

1.     Create a new RTCPeerConnection with an iceServers list that contains at least one STUN server.

2.     Loop over the local stream's tracks and call addTrack() for each one.

3.     In the ontrack handler, attach the first remote stream to your remote video element.

4.     In the onicecandidate handler, send every new candidate to the other peer through your signalling channel as a candidate message.

One peer creates an offer, the other answers, and both apply each other's descriptions. Candidates arrive asynchronously in the background.

1.     The caller runs createOffer(), applies it with setLocalDescription() and sends the description as an offer message.

2.     The callee applies the offer with setRemoteDescription(), runs createAnswer(), applies it locally and sends it back as an answer.

3.     The caller applies the answer with setRemoteDescription().

4.     Both sides pass every incoming candidate message to addIceCandidate().

If both peers might start a call at the same time, look up the "perfect negotiation" pattern on MDN. It assigns one side as polite and resolves offer collisions cleanly instead of leaving both peers stuck.

On a home network, STUN alone often works. On corporate Wi-Fi, mobile carriers and symmetric NATs it frequently does not, and the call silently fails to connect. A TURN server relays media when a direct path is impossible. The open-source coturn server is the usual choice.

In practice, your iceServers list should contain your own STUN URL plus a TURN URL with a username and credential, so the browser can fall back to the relay automatically when direct connectivity fails.

Generate short-lived TURN credentials on your backend rather than hard-coding them, and budget for bandwidth: relayed calls pass every frame through your server.

Users deny camera access, unplug headsets and switch from Wi-Fi to 4G mid-call. Catch NotAllowedError and show a clear explanation, listen for devicechange events, and use RTCRtpSender.replaceTrack() to swap cameras without renegotiating the whole call.

Listen to pc.onconnectionstatechange. When the state becomes disconnected, wait a few seconds before giving up, because it often recovers. On failed, call pc.restartIce() to try new network paths.

Use RTCRtpSender.setParameters() to cap the bitrate on weak connections, and read pc.getStats() to monitor packet loss and round-trip time. Showing a small "poor connection" indicator does more for perceived quality than any codec tweak.

Mesh calls, where every participant connects to every other, work for two or three people. Beyond that, upload bandwidth explodes. Group calls need a Selective Forwarding Unit (SFU) such as mediasoup, LiveKit or Janus, which receives each stream once and forwards it to the others.

Tutorials end at a working demo, but the fastest way to understand what production WebRTC looks like is to watch a real call from the inside. Chrome ships an internal diagnostics page that shows every peer connection the browser opens, and it works on any site that uses WebRTC.

1.     Open chrome://webrtc-internals in one tab and keep it open.

2.     In another tab, start a call on your own demo and find its RTCPeerConnection entry.

3.     Expand that entry and look at the ICE candidate pairs: you will see whether the call went direct (host or srflx) or through a TURN relay (relay).

4.     Open the stats graphs for the outbound video stream and watch the bitrate, frame rate and resolution adapt when you throttle the network in DevTools.

5.     Check the negotiated codec and how quickly the connection recovers when you switch Wi-Fi off and back on.

Once you can read your own call, repeat the exercise on a production service. Any browser-based product works, whether a meeting tool or a live video chat that pairs two visitors, because webrtc-internals shows the same data for every site. Expect differences from your demo: more candidate pairs, a relay candidate in the list and a bitrate that adapts faster. Seeing them makes the earlier sections concrete, from why TURN matters to how bandwidth estimation reacts to packet loss.

The one-to-one call above is the core of many products, from telehealth consultations and tutoring to customer support. The engineering challenges grow with the use case: random matching needs queueing and presence on the signalling server, public platforms need moderation and reporting, and global audiences need TURN servers in several regions.

Start with the minimal version, get it working across Chrome, Firefox and Safari on both desktop and mobile, and add TURN before real users arrive. Once the call is reliable, every other feature becomes a much easier problem.

#10282. Defense firm’s EMP ‘Dragon’ weapon fries 51 jam-proof drones simultaneously

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Drones have become a major menace on modern battlefields, which is why Turkish defense firm Aselsan has developed a potent solution to counter them. The company shared a YouTube video showing its Ejderha, which translates to “Dragon” in English Anti-Drone Countermeasure System taking down 51 drones simultaneously. The firm says that the device uses high-power electromagnetic (HPEM) waves and vacuum electronics to disable the electronic components on mini and micro drones.

While drones and uncrewed aerial vehicles (UAVs) have been around for decades now, Russia’s 2022 escalation in Ukraine saw the first widespread use of small drones on the front line, some of which have been adapted from commercial models. Because of this, we’ve seen various innovations in drone technologies, including the deployment of fiber optic drones to defeat radio jamming, the use of an onboard camera looking at a magnetic compass as a backup for GPS navigation, as well as the introduction of jam-resistant strike drones and “flying modems” that relay signals from one drone to another to extend their strike range.

Many countries have started working on anti-drone defenses, too. Ukraine has erected mobile barriers with rotating barbed wire designed to cut the fiber-optic lines that jam-proof Russian drones rely on, while various companies in the U.S.—from established players like Lockheed Martin and Northrop Grumman to startups like Mara—have been working on both airborne and ground-based solutions. China has also unveiled a man-portable design that neutralizes drones in under four seconds at up to 1,600 feet away using a 2-kilowatt laser.

Before the invention of the transistor in 1947, electronic devices relied on vacuum tubes, which were invented in 1904 by British engineer John Ambrose Fleming. Today, most devices rely on semiconductors for their power efficiency, reliability, and computing density. But if you need to produce a microwave signal strong enough to overwhelm the electronic components inside a drone, vacuum electronics is still one of the most effective solutions (although some companies are experimenting with solid-state semiconductors, too).

The U.S. and the UK are also working on their own HPEM systems, with the former testing the Leonidas Autonomous Ground Vehicle (AGV) and the latter trialing the Radio Frequency Directed Energy Weapon (RFDEW).

#10280. Building Email Integrations: APIs, Webhooks, Automation, and Deliverability

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Email remains an important communication channel for web applications. From account verification and password resets to product announcements and customer notifications, many applications depend on email to keep users informed.

For developers, however, implementing email functionality involves more than connecting a service and sending a message. A reliable email system needs to handle authentication, dynamic content, delivery failures, user preferences, event tracking, and integrations with other application components.

Using an email service provider (ESP) can simplify this process. Most modern providers offer REST APIs, SMTP relays, templates, automation features, and reporting tools that developers can integrate into existing applications.

This guide explores the technical considerations involved in building email integrations, including API-based sending, webhook handling, automation workflows, and deliverability.

There are two common ways to connect an application to an email delivery service: SMTP and REST APIs.

SMTP (Simple Mail Transfer Protocol) is a standard protocol used to transfer email messages between servers. Developers can connect an application to an SMTP server using credentials and configuration settings.

REST APIs provide another approach. Instead of communicating through an email server protocol, an application sends HTTP requests to an endpoint. The provider processes the request and returns a response.

Both methods can support transactional and application-generated emails, but their implementation differs.

An application already supports SMTP configuration.

Developers want a relatively straightforward integration.

Existing frameworks or libraries provide built-in SMTP support.

An application needs programmatic control over email operations.

Developers want structured responses and error handling.

Email functionality needs to integrate with other application services.

The system requires event tracking or additional API capabilities.

The right approach depends on the application's architecture, expected email volume, and integration requirements.

An API integration allows an application to trigger emails in response to specific events.

For example, an ecommerce application might send an order confirmation after a successful payment. A SaaS application could send an account verification message after registration.

A typical integration follows this process:

The backend prepares the recipient and message data.

The backend authenticates with the email provider.

The application records the response and handles any errors.

Consider a Node.js application that needs to trigger an email campaign through an API.

Some platforms, including Mailmodo, provide REST API functionality for triggering campaigns and integrating external applications with email workflows.

The following example demonstrates the general structure of an HTTP request using a campaign-triggering endpoint:

This example uses the documented Mailmodo trigger endpoint and request structure. The campaign ID and API key must be replaced with the values from the relevant account.

Notice that the API key is loaded from an environment variable rather than written directly into the source code.

This is an important security practice. Credentials should never be exposed in frontend JavaScript, public repositories, or client-side application bundles.

Not every email provider offers the same integration capabilities. Developers should evaluate providers based on their API documentation, authentication methods, supported endpoints, event notifications, and integration flexibility.

For example, Elastic Email provides a REST API that developers can use to send emails and manage different email operations from an application.

Its API uses HTTPS requests and API-key authentication. Developers can use the available endpoints to send messages, manage campaigns, retrieve information, and configure webhook notifications.

When evaluating an email API, consider these technical requirements:

Authentication: Check how API keys are generated, stored, restricted, and rotated.

Documentation: Look for clear endpoint descriptions, request examples, response formats, and error codes.

SDK availability: Official libraries can reduce the amount of integration code required.

Permission management: Separate credentials for sending emails and accessing account information can reduce unnecessary exposure.

API limitations: Review connection limits, request timeouts, rate limits, and account-level restrictions before deploying an integration.

A provider with extensive features is not necessarily the best option for every project. The important consideration is whether its API supports the application's actual requirements.

Sending an email is only one part of the communication process. Applications may also need to know what happens after a message is submitted.

A webhook allows an external service to send an HTTP notification to an application when a particular event occurs.

Depending on the provider, events may include:

For example, an application could receive a delivery-failure notification and update the corresponding email record in its database.

A basic Express.js webhook endpoint might look like this:

This is a simplified example rather than a complete provider-specific webhook implementation. A production endpoint should validate the provider's signature or other authentication mechanism and follow its documented payload structure.

It should also handle duplicate events safely. Webhook providers may retry notifications when an application does not respond successfully.

An idempotent event handler prevents the same notification from creating duplicate database updates or triggering repeated actions.

Email automation connects application events with predefined communication sequences.

Instead of manually sending every message, developers can create workflows that respond to user activity.

Consider a SaaS application with the following registration process:

If the user does not complete onboarding, a follow-up message may be triggered.

A simplified workflow can be represented as:

In a production application, email delivery should generally be handled through a background job queue rather than blocking the registration request.

A queue also makes it easier to retry temporary failures, manage sending volume, and prevent email delays from affecting the main application.

Developers should consider tools such as Redis-backed queues or framework-specific job processors when building larger systems.

Automation platforms can also help manage more complex journeys. For instance, a developer can connect application events to an external email workflow instead of implementing every communication sequence independently.

The key is to keep business logic in the application while using the email service to manage message delivery and relevant campaign operations.

A successful API response does not necessarily mean that an email has reached the recipient's inbox.

Email deliverability depends on several technical factors, including domain authentication, sender reputation, message quality, and recipient engagement.

Developers should pay attention to the following configurations.

Sender Policy Framework identifies the mail servers authorized to send messages on behalf of a domain.

A correctly configured SPF record helps receiving servers evaluate whether an email originates from an authorized source.

DomainKeys Identified Mail adds a cryptographic signature to outgoing messages. Receiving servers can use the corresponding public key in DNS to verify the signature.

Domain-based Message Authentication, Reporting, and Conformance builds on SPF and DKIM. It allows domain owners to publish policies for handling messages that fail authentication checks and receive reports about authentication activity.

These three mechanisms should be configured carefully, especially when an application sends emails through multiple providers.

Email systems generate useful operational data. However, developers should avoid treating every available metric as an accurate measure of user engagement.

More reliable application-level signals can include:

These events can be connected with email campaign data to understand how communication affects application activity.

Developers should also maintain a clear separation between email delivery records and user account records. This makes it easier to investigate delivery issues without unnecessarily modifying customer data.

Before deploying an email integration, developers should review the following areas:

API security: Store credentials in environment variables or a dedicated secrets manager.

Domain authentication: Configure SPF, DKIM, and DMARC.

Error handling: Account for failed requests, invalid recipients, and temporary provider errors.

Background processing: Use queues for asynchronous email delivery.

Webhook validation: Verify incoming notifications and handle duplicate events.

Monitoring: Track delivery failures, bounce rates, and application-level outcomes.

Testing: Test email templates across different clients and devices before production deployment.

These practices help create an email system that is easier to maintain, troubleshoot, and scale.

Email integration is an important part of modern application development, but a reliable implementation requires more than connecting an SMTP server or calling an API endpoint.

Developers need to consider authentication, asynchronous processing, webhook security, automation logic, domain configuration, and event monitoring.

REST APIs are useful for applications that need programmatic control over email operations, while SMTP remains a practical option for systems that already support standard mail-server connections. Webhooks add another layer by allowing applications to respond to delivery events and update their internal records.

By choosing an integration method that fits the application architecture and following sound security and deliverability practices, developers can build email functionality that supports both immediate communication needs and long-term application growth.

Launched as part of the Drone as First Responder program, the drones can be deployed within seconds of a 911 call being received.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The Knoxville Police Department has announced its “Drone as First Responder” program, or DFR, with semi-autonomous drones set to be launched from 12 docks located around the city. The docks will allow drones to be dispatched within seconds of a 911 call being received, and they will be able to reach nearly any area of the city within just 90 seconds, according to a page on the police department's website. It's hoped that the drones will be able to reduce the risk to the community as well as police officers, while providing real-time situational awareness during "rapidly evolving events."

While the police department hasn't confirmed which drones will be used, we do know that they will be launched from Skydio docks located at city-owned facilities. It's expected that the drones will, in some cases, reach the scene more quickly than the police officer they are supposed to accompany. This should give officers vital information related to the scene that they are about to attend.

Knoxville isn't the first to turn to drones in this way. Several cities were reportedly experimenting with using Flock's own DFR system last month. Companies have also been advertising Starlink-connected first responder drones for some time.

Among the new features of Android 17 are improvements to the system interface, with more customization options, new icons, and changes to the layout of buttons and widgets. The Settings menu has been redesigned to make navigation more intuitive and facilitate access to the smartphone’s main functions.

With the update, Circle to Search now offers real-time translations, allowing you to understand content in other languages ​​more easily, without having to interrupt your current activity on your smartphone.

Security remains a top priority for the system. The Authentication Failure Lock , a security feature in case of theft, locks the screen after unsuccessful authentication attempts. Meanwhile, Message-Based Fraud Detection enhances the identification of potential threats and fraud. Furthermore, access to emergency calls has become easier and faster.

The update also improves HDR brightness control, which adjusts the screen intensity to highlight bright details in compatible photos and videos. Users can adjust this brightness for more comfortable viewing, or even disable the feature.

The Motorola Signature 27, the company’s new flagship, ships with Android 17 out of the box.

The Gigabyte MO27Q2A is a superlative value among 27-inch QHD OLEDs with high performance, accurate color and premium build quality. It matches or exceeds many more expensive monitors.

Tactical Switch for quick resolution changes

Excellent build quality with mostly metal construction

Why you can trust Tom's Hardware Our expert reviewers spend hours testing and comparing products and services so you can choose the best for you. Find out more about how we test.

I have long said that choices are important, and nowhere is that better illustrated than in the best OLED gaming monitor category. Once an exotic and expensive technology, it is now much more affordable, thanks to a plethora of new models coming to market.

Gigabyte is certainly a leader in the price war with new models that deliver tremendous performance, image quality, and value. Its latest effort is a 27-inch QHD OLED model that refreshes at 280 Hz. It’s the MO27Q2A, and it leaves nothing out in its quest to be fast, beautiful, and packed with features. You get Adaptive-Sync with G-Sync and FreeSync certification, wide gamut color courtesy of Quantum Dot tech, HDR10, VESA DisplayHDR 400 True Black, USB ports with KVM, internal speakers and a better-than-average stand. Let’s take a look.

Quantum Dot Organic Light Emitting Diode (QD-OLED)

If there’s one thing that the MO27Q2A leaves out, it's LED lighting. There’s no colorful show to dazzle your opponents with. But nothing else has been sacrificed. There are HDMI 2.1 ports, DisplayPort 1.4 with DSC (Display Stream Compression), USB-C that supports video and 18 watts of power, plus USB Type-A and Type-B ports with KVM control. A pair of five-watt internal speakers delivers good audio, or you can use the 3.5mm headphone jack.

The screen is super colorful with over 108% coverage of DCI-P3 thanks to a Quantum Dot layer. Color is very accurate, so much so that I found calibration completely unnecessary. You get sRGB and Adobe RGB options as well. Brightness is solid at 250 nits for a full field and over 1,000 nits for a 3% window pattern. You can use variable brightness for SDR and HDR content to pump up small highlights for even more dynamic range.

High-performance gaming is the primary goal here, and for that, the MO27Q2A comes with a reliable 280 Hz refresh rate, Adaptive-Sync with certifications from Nvidia and AMD, and a host of Game Assist features like aiming points, sniper modes, flash suppression, timers, and a frame counter. Like all OLEDs, it has lightning-fast reflexes with super smooth motion rendering.

Gigabyte pays plenty of attention to panel care with a host of options like pixel refresh and logo detection. There’s extensive passive cooling (no fans) and AI control that tracks usage at the pixel level to keep everything in balance. The end result is longevity and reliability, which is backed by a three-year factory warranty that includes burn-in.

The MO27Q2A’s value is the most impressive part. At this writing, it sells for $399 online. It wasn’t long ago that you’d pay that for a 240 Hz QHD LCD. That category has dropped in cost as well, with some screens coming in around $200. But spending an extra $200 for an OLED seems like a no-brainer to me. Let’s see how the MO27Q2A fares in my tests.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Gigabyte offers a bit of fanfare on the MO27Q2A’s carton with colored graphics that set it apart from the plain brown wrappers more commonly seen at the big box store. A clamshell lid opens to reveal blocks of crumbly foam containing the panel, upright, and base, which assemble without tools. The power supply is internal, so you get a selection of IEC cords plus cables for HDMI, DisplayPort, and USB. A little clip goes on the stand to tidy up the wiring.

The panel is minimalist from the front with a thin flush bezel and a metal backing that wraps around the edges. A narrow trim strip crosses the bottom with a tiny Gigabyte logo in its center. The controls are under the center point and consist of a backlit joystick and two keys. One is the Tactical Switch, and it changes resolutions on the fly. More about that below.

The stand is above average in quality with all-metal construction and smooth ergonomics. It includes 5.2 inches of height range plus 5/21 degrees tilt, 15 degrees swivel, and a 90-degree portrait mode. There is no hint of wobble or play. It is one of the better stands I’ve encountered, and I’ve encountered many.

From the side, you can see the panel-on-bulge design that is shared by many OLEDs. Not only does this look cool, but it actually is cool. The MO27Q2A’s panel is backed by metal, which acts as a heat sink. The bulge is also well ventilated, enough so that no fans are needed. A graphene layer behind the OLED contributes to cooling and hence, longevity.

Underneath is a well-appointed I/O panel with two HDMI 2.1s, a DisplayPort 1.4 with DSC, a USB-C that supports video and 18 watts of power, and USB 3.2, one upstream and two down. They can be bound to different video inputs using the KVM feature in the OSD. You also get a 3.5mm headphone jack and a pair of internal speakers with five watts of amplification.

The MO27Q2A’s OSD presents complete information with signal status at the top and current settings shown at the bottom.

The Gaming menu includes a black frame insertion feature called Ultra Clear. It works at 280 or 120 Hz and is effective at removing motion blur. If you can run at 180fps or higher, you won’t need it. Other options include Black Equalizer, which increases shadow visibility, super resolution (edge enhancement, do not use), display mode for aspect ratio control, and a VRR toggle.

The MO27Q2A includes 10 picture modes mainly aimed at different game types. It comes out of the box in Eco, which limits brightness. Choose Standard to unlock the maximum light output. Within this mode, you can adjust color temp and gamma and choose a color space. My sample was nearly perfect out of the box, so good, in fact, that I could not improve it with calibration. If you want sRGB or Adobe RGB color, those choices are in the Color Space submenu. For sRGB, there’s an sRGB picture mode. HDR signals get their own five modes. Peak 1000 is the brightest and best choice there.

You can use the KVM Wizard to bind USB ports and video inputs. That way, one keyboard, mouse, and monitor can service multiple sources like a PC and a gaming console, for instance. You can also view two sources at once using the PIP/PBP options.

Quick Switch lets the user program the functions of the joystick directionals. It provides quick access to commonly used functions like brightness or crosshairs.

A separate screen comes up for the MO27Q2A’s OLED care options. This is where you’ll find APL Stabilize, which has two levels of variable brightness or a constant setting. Its terminology is counterintuitive because Low means off, while Middle and High modulate light output more aggressively. High has the most dynamic range, with 1,000 peaks possible in HDR mode. If it were up to me, APL Stabilize would be in the Picture menu and be called Variable Brightness. Additional care options include pixel orbiting, panel refresh, and dimming for logos and corners. You turn any or all of these off if you wish.

Press the joystick and click right for the Game Assist menu and its plethora of play aids. You get four crosshairs, a timer, stopwatch, refresh rate indicator, and positioning of the information window. Also included are sniper mode and night vision. Flash Dimming suppresses your weapon’s muzzle flash if that becomes a distraction.

Gigabyte’s Tactical Switch is a unique feature that switches the MO27Q2A’s resolution on the fly. You can have FHD or HD in 16:9, 4:3, or 5:4 aspect ratios. The refresh rate remains at 280 Hz, but by shrinking the image, you get higher frame rates with no significant sharpness penalty.

There isn’t much to the MO27Q2A’s calibration because it isn’t necessary. In fact, I could not improve my sample with calibration. It measures nearly perfect out of the box for D65, 2.2 gamma, and coverage of DCI-P3. The sRGB mode, which is also very accurate, is found in the picture mode list. For Adobe RGB, go to the Color Space sub-menu. For gaming and daily use, I settled on Standard mode, Native color space, and left everything else at default settings. My recommendations are below.

For HDR signals, HDR is the default mode, but I found the same excellent accuracy and high brightness in the Peak 1000 mode. It delivers the promised 1,000 nits for a 3% window, which in my tests equated to 445 nits for a 25% window pattern. You get a number of sliders for brightness and contrast, but they are not needed unless you want a different look for HDR content.

A great way to evaluate a monitor’s HDR accuracy is by viewing static content that covers the full dynamic range of both color and neutral tones. Doom Eternal has such a screen in its HDR adjustment menu, and I use it for every review to verify detail rendering. The MO27Q2A showed every nuance in this torture test, which told me that it is very color accurate and tracks luminance correctly. In simple terms, you’ll see everything the game creators put in.

This gives gameplay a heightened sense of realism, especially in textures like stone, metal, and dirt. The MO27Q2A let me almost reach out and touch them. I had to remind myself that its resolution was QHD and Ultra HD because it rendered so finely. I also saw rich color that was perfectly balanced. You’ll see in the SDR and HDR tests that this is a seriously precise monitor.

Gameplay was up to the premium OLED standard with smooth motion that was completely free of blur. I had no trouble maintaining a constant 280fps. If you can’t hit that speed, Gigabyte includes Ultra Clear, which is a black frame insertion feature. It will remove motion blur at lower rates like 120 Hz. I noted that it was available at 280 Hz as well, which is unusual. It cuts brightness by around 40%, but you can partially compensate for that with the brightness slider. It operated artifact-free.

Another way to increase frame rates is with the Tactical Switch, which changes resolution and picture size on the fly. It doesn’t increase the refresh rate, but by shrinking the image, it maintains a favorable pixel map so that sharpness doesn’t take a huge hit.

Gameplay was addictive during my numerous frag fests. Input lag and response are super quick, which helped make my aim more precise. The MO27Q2A was so fast that it made me overconfident. I rushed into the melee rather than picking off enemies one by one. It was truly difficult to stop playing.

The MO27Q2A excels as an everyday screen with 109ppi pixel density in its 27-inch form factor. It’s significantly less expensive than an Ultra HD monitor if you can accept a small reduction in clarity. And that resolution is a sweet spot for frame rates. You won’t need a giga-hauler video card to achieve its full potential.

Accurate color out of the box meant I could rely on the MO27Q2A as a reference for graphics and photos. I found excellent results in my initial tests, and I could not improve upon them with adjustments. This monitor is pro-level for sure.

It’s impressive ergonomically too, with a solid all-metal stand and rugged panel construction. There are no missing features except for flashy LEDs. The internal speakers play loud and clear in the upper midrange, or you can plug in headphones. Users with a PC and console will appreciate the USB ports and KVM functions.

Takeaway: The MO27Q2A is a truly full-featured gaming monitor that can double as an everyday screen or a professional display. It games on par with the very best screens I’ve reviewed and delivers performance equal to more expensive OLEDs with higher refresh rates. It also matches those products in build quality with a solid stand and metal construction. For $399, you’ll be hard-pressed to find better.

#10266. What is Pathways? Single-controller training for large models

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Today, we announce the General Availability of Pathways on Google Cloud Platform. In this post, you will learn about Pathways, the distributed system Google built to train its own large models, including Gemini. We discuss how Pathways addresses the coordination challenge by introducing one client that describes the whole computation as a graph, and how the distributed runtime places it, moves the tensors, and keeps the accelerators busy.

Most large-model training runs as Single Program, Multiple Data (SPMD) programs. Every accelerator runs the same program in lockstep and coordinates through collectives like AllReduce.

It works well when your job is the same program running on every device. It struggles when the job is no longer uniform, like a pipeline whose stages wait on each other or an RL loop where a sampler, a reward model, and a trainer are different programs that have to overlap.

Large runs also have to survive hardware failures, which interrupted Gemini 2.5 training multiple times per hour. In SPMD, one lost machine stops every process and sends the run back to its last checkpoint. In each case, the challenge is coordination. You have to decide what runs where and in what order, how tensors move between devices, and how the job keeps going when a machine drops out.

In this post of the series “Pathways on Google Cloud”, you will learn about Pathways, the distributed system Google built to train its own large models, including Gemini. The post discusses how Pathways addresses the coordination challenge by introducing one client that describes the whole computation as a graph, and how the distributed runtime places it, moves the tensors, and keeps the accelerators busy.

By the end, you'll know where SPMD is suboptimal, what single-controller execution changes, and how Pathways coordinates a whole training job across a cluster.

If you train PyTorch models, you almost certainly use Distributed Data Parallel (DDP) or Fully Sharded Data Parallel (FSDP). DDP runs a copy of your script on every accelerator and averages gradients with a collective operation such as AllReduce. FSDP shards parameters, gradients, and optimizer states across workers so that the model fits at all.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

DDP and FSDP both follow the Single Program, Multiple Data (SPMD) execution model. Every process runs the same copy of your script, owns its accelerators for the whole run, and moves in lockstep with the others, because the only way they coordinate is through collectives like AllReduce. Inside one tightly connected group of accelerators those collectives run over a fast interconnect and are cheap. Scale the job across groups and the same collectives cross the datacenter network, which is far slower. On a TPU v5p, for example, each chip gets 1,200 GB/s of bidirectional chip-to-chip bandwidth inside its group, but only 50 Gb/s (gigabits, about 6 GB/s) to the datacenter network which is roughly two orders of magnitude less.

And because every step blocks on its collectives, that communication lands directly in step time. The more groups you span, the larger the share of each step spent moving data, so throughput grows slower than the number of accelerators you add.

As long as your training job is one program that fills the hardware you give it, this execution model fits well. You write your model as if there were one device, and the collectives do the rest. But fitting a model into memory is only part of the problem. Keeping every accelerator busy is the harder part, and this is where the SPMD model starts to show its limits.

Take pipeline parallelism as an example. You split a model into blocks across devices, and each device now runs different code over smaller micro-batches. Activations flow forward, activation gradients flow back, and while device 0 works on the first micro-batch, devices 1 and 2 wait. Apart from the idle accelerators you keep paying for, this drops utilization and throughput, even though every device has work in the steady state.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Another example is RL post-training for an LLM. You have a sampler that generates rollouts, a reward model that scores them, and a trainer that updates the policy and sends the new weights back to the sampler. These are three different programs that should overlap; otherwise the sampler is idle while the trainer runs, and the trainer is idle while the sampler generates.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

In both scenarios, the job is now several different programs running on different devices, and their dependencies can no longer be managed through a single collective. You now have to coordinate which devices share which tensors, which processes run while others are still finishing, and how early to enqueue the next buffer so an accelerator never waits for input. Because each worker only has visibility into its local devices and data, no individual process can manage all of these dependencies itself. That has several consequences.

First, because each process owns its accelerators for the whole run and every collective blocks until all devices finish, one failed machine prevents the collective from completing and stops the entire job, forcing a restart from the last checkpoint. You cannot add devices mid-run, and you cannot give devices back.

Second, because each process holds its accelerators from start to finish, capacity can't move to where it's needed. Within one job, a stage that needs fewer chips than another still keeps the extra ones. They sit idle, you keep paying for them, and no other part of the job can use them until the run ends. Across a team, the same happens at cluster scale. Every job owns its accelerators for as long as it runs, so sharing a cluster means splitting it into per-job allocations, and each new job brings up its own processes on its own hosts.

Finally, the development process itself gets harder. On one host you iterate fast because you run the script, drop into a Python shell, set a breakpoint, and debug. This is not true once the job spans many hosts. You need a launcher to push your script to every host, you hit coordination bugs that never showed up on one machine, and there's no single process to break into. When something hangs, each worker is stuck at a collective waiting for the others, with no one place that shows the whole job.

So the common challenge here is the coordination itself. As soon as the job stops being one program in lockstep, you have to decide what runs where, in what order, and how tensors get from one device to the next.

One way to describe this is as a graph, where each block is a node, each tensor that moves between blocks is an edge, and the devices are where the nodes are placed. You can wire that graph up by hand in SPMD, one process at a time. But what if a single client declared that graph, including what each block does, what it needs, and where it runs, and a distributed runtime handled the transfers, dependencies, and ordering along its edges?

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

That is the idea behind Pathways, the distributed system Google built to train its own largest models, including Gemini. Pathways runs JAX programs on TPUs and it is available on Google Cloud.

With Pathways, you define the end-to-end computation from a single Python client, without writing custom coordination code, and a distributed runtime executes it. That client is decoupled from the hardware. It runs on an ordinary CPU machine and you write your program against virtual devices; a resource manager maps them onto real chips, and a worker on each accelerator host runs the compiled programs it receives.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

Because your code refers to virtual devices rather than physical ones, the runtime is flexible to allow users to change the devices used for a given computation. This allows you to stay in control while the runtime handles the mapping to hardware. In an RL loop, for example, the trainer and the samplers rarely want the same number of chips, so you can give the sampler stage more chips by asking for more virtual devices.

Also, the same capability helps when a host fails or the hardware isn't ready yet. If a host dies, the resource manager remaps the work onto healthy chips and the run keeps going instead of relaunching the whole job, which is the basis for elastic training. And because the program targets virtual devices, compilation can start before the physical chips are provisioned, so setup overlaps with provisioning instead of waiting on it.

The resource manager owning the chips also means one Pathways cluster can serve several clients, so researchers on a team can share its capacity. With the Shared Pathways Service, the cluster stays up as a long running service, which lets you develop interactively on TPUs hosted on GKE. Each client connects on demand from a laptop or VM, gets its own slices (groups of tightly connected TPU chips) from the shared pool, and hands them back when it disconnects. When every chip is busy, the next workload waits until capacity frees up.

Take the three-block model from Figure 2. Block A runs on one group of devices, block B on a second, block C on a third. You declare what each block computes, its target devices, and where its outputs go. Pathways expresses those dependencies as a distributed dataflow graph, in which each block is a compiled SPMD program and each edge is a tensor the runtime has to deliver.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

In a single SPMD program, the framework handles the collectives that keep the devices in sync within a group, with or without Pathways, and your code doesn't change. The difference shows up once the job runs several programs on different groups (Multiple Programs, Multiple Data, MPMD), like these three blocks or the sampler, reward model, and trainer of an RL loop. Then no single process sees both sides (the same limit from before, where each worker only knows its own devices), so getting a tensor from block A to block B is a hand-off you have to coordinate yourself.

Pathways closes that gap. You declare the dependency once, and the runtime moves and reshards the tensor for you, over the fast interconnect within a group and over the datacenter network between groups.

To keep the accelerators busy, Pathways prepares launches ahead of time with futures and parallel asynchronous dispatch. While block A is still computing, block B can't run yet, but its input shapes and dtypes are already known, so the runtime allocates B's buffers and schedules the transfer before A's output even exists. Host-side setup for every block happens up front, and each block launches when its inputs arrive.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

This is also what keeps the single client from becoming a bottleneck. Because dispatch is asynchronous, the coordination work overlaps with the compute already running on the chips, so the client stays off the critical path instead of stalling the accelerators between steps.

In Pathways, the runtime decides execution order when a group has more than one operation ready. In pipelined training, B's devices often hold both the forward pass for an upcoming micro-batch and the backward pass for an earlier one. If each device picked on its own, their collectives would conflict and the group would stall.

Pathways has a central scheduler that puts every program's operations into one consistent order, so operations that must communicate start together instead of deadlocking.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

And because it's still one client, the development loop we mentioned before comes back. It's the same single Python process whether you drive one host or thousands of hosts, so you can drop into a shell, set a breakpoint, and inspect the run as it goes. When something stalls, there's one place that knows what's queued on every device and which dependency hasn't been met, instead of a hang spread across separate processes.

Put together, Pathways separates model building from execution. You choose how the model partitions across hardware using the client. And the runtime handles the transfers, ordering, and scheduling that keep accelerators busy, across the whole cluster rather than just your slice of it.

Described this way, Pathways can start to sound like an orchestrator you already know. If scheduling work, moving tensors, and keeping a whole cluster busy brings Ray to mind, it should. But the two work at different layers.

Ray is an open-source framework for orchestrating Python-native AI workloads across large clusters. It provisions the cluster, schedules your Python code as tasks and actors, and launches your workload across hosts, holding data loading, training, serving, and tuning under one control plane. To Ray, an accelerator is a resource to schedule.

What Ray doesn't change is how your training runs on the chips. The job inside each worker still runs as SPMD. The framework inserts the collectives, and each process sees only its own devices. Ray places and starts that job but it doesn't turn it into a single program that drives the whole cluster.

That is the layer Pathways works at. It changes the execution model underneath, so one client coordinates every chip. Because they sit at different layers, the two are complementary. In principle, Ray can orchestrate the cluster and the pipeline while Pathways handles how the training step executes.

So concretely, Ray's job is about orchestration while Pathways' job is about execution. With Ray, you tie your data, training, serving, and tuning together and spread heterogeneous work across accelerators and clouds. With Pathways, you run one model across many groups of chips, express patterns beyond SPMD, and recover from a failed host instead of relaunching the whole job.

When a training job stops being one uniform program, the challenge becomes coordinating the dependencies between stages, the tensors that cross the network, and the ordering that keeps every accelerator fed.

SPMD requires you to address these dependencies. With Pathways, you describe the computation once, from a single client, as a graph of blocks and the tensors between them. And you have a runtime that places the blocks on virtual devices, moves and reshards the tensors, orders the work across the cluster, and remaps around a failed host.

With Pathways, model building and execution are separated and you reason about the whole cluster instead of one slice of it.

If you are curious to learn more about Pathways, you can take a look at the following resources:

How to handle user, connector, and agent auth in Gemini Enterprise with a custom UI

Agent Anomaly Detection: Safeguarding agents across other clouds

How to securely connect internal Cloud Run MCP servers to Gemini Enterprise

Check what your AI agents do before they do it, with Semantic governance policies
