#10265. How to Test WCAG 2.2 in CI: What axe-core Misses

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

Your accessibility check in CI is green. axe-core ran, zero violations, and the badge in the README looks great. Your site can still fail WCAG 2.2.

That isn't a bug in axe. WCAG 2.2 became a W3C Recommendation in October 2023. It added nine success criteria and dropped one (4.1.1 Parsing), and most of the new ones can't be judged by reading the DOM. Deque said as much when it shipped the first WCAG 2.2 support in axe-core 4.5: the target-size rule would probably be the only new-criterion rule they added, because the others can't be automated without a lot of false positives.

So the rest is on you. This article covers the one criterion axe can check and three it can't (focus not obscured, dragging movements, accessible authentication), with Playwright tests you can drop into a project. I ran all of them against a small test page that fails every criterion on purpose, and against a fixed version of the same page. If you want a plain-language rundown of every change first, WCAG 2.2 vs 2.1 goes through them. Here I only care about what you can put in a pipeline.

Level A and AA are what most contracts and laws point at, so those are the ones that matter first. The AAA criteria are still worth knowing about.

That's four AA criteria with something you can script, one of which axe already handles. The rest of this article goes through those four.

The AA rule is smaller than most people expect: a pointer target should be at least 24 by 24 CSS pixels, or sit far enough from its neighbours that a 24px circle centred on it wouldn't overlap another target or that target's own circle. There are exceptions, including inline links in a sentence, and targets whose size the browser controls.

axe-core has a target-size rule for this. One catch: it isn't part of a default run. In my setup (axe-core 4.14.1 through @axe-core/playwright 4.14.0), a plain analyze() on the broken page reported nothing at all. The rule only showed up once I asked for it:

On the broken page this lists the three 16px toolbar buttons by selector. On the fixed page, with 32px buttons, it passes. If you've been running axe for years and never seen a target-size result, this is why.

Some context on how common the problem is. In the scan data behind my last SitePoint article, 34.5% of the 1,025 sites had controls below a stricter 44px threshold. That is a tougher standard than the 24px that AA asks for, so don't read it as "a third of sites fail 2.5.8". Plenty of those would pass. But it does show how many production sites ship tiny controls.

If you'd like a quick look at one page before wiring anything up, browser tools such as axe DevTools, WAVE, or a WCAG checker will show the same thing.

axe can't test it, because you have to press Tab and look at where the browser scrolled. Two details caught me out building this:

The check below samples a grid of points inside the visible part of the focused element and asks the browser what's on top at each one. If none of the points land on the element or its children, it's covered.

On my broken page it reported nine hits: links hidden behind the footer going forward ("Section 4 link", "Section 10 link", "Section 16 link") and the toolbar buttons and a few links hidden behind the header coming back. The output names the element and the key, which is what you want when you're staring at a failed build.

The fix is one CSS rule, sized to your sticky elements:

That tells the browser to leave room when it scrolls a focused element into view. With it in place the same test passes.

Be aware of what this can't see: a modal that opens over the page, or a chat widget that only appears after a delay, will not show up unless your test opens or waits for them. Adrian Roselli's piece on 2.4.11 and adversarial conformance is worth reading for the awkward cases.

Anything you can do by dragging needs a way to do it with a single click or tap, unless dragging is essential. Sortable lists, sliders, kanban cards, file reorderers: all of them.

You can't prove a drag alternative works from a script. But you can catch the obvious mistake, which is a draggable item with no button anywhere near it:

The limit is real. It only recognises the native draggable attribute. Libraries built on pointer events, such as dnd-kit, don't set it, so this test will walk right past them. If you use one of those, replace the selector with whatever your component renders, or add a test per drag component. Treat it as a tripwire for the common case, not a verdict.

The fix, in the fixed page, is a pair of "Move up" and "Move down" buttons on each item, each with an accessible name that says which item it moves.

You can't make someone solve a cognitive test to log in (remember a password, transcribe a code) unless there's an alternative or a mechanism that helps. Password managers count as that mechanism, which means the two ways sites break it are blocking paste and omitting the right autocomplete values.

This dispatches a synthetic paste event and checks whether anything called preventDefault() on it. It catches the old onpaste="return false" trick, and it catches script-based blockers too. It does not tell you whether your login has a CAPTCHA that needs an alternative, or an emailed one-time code you must retype. Those need a person.

On a pull request, a plain GitHub Actions job is enough:

Point PAGE_URL at a running preview, and run the tests against the pages that matter most: sign-in, checkout, anything with a sticky element. Don't try to crawl everything on the first pass. A test that fails for reasons nobody owns gets deleted within a month.

Four things I haven't tried to automate, and wouldn't:

Automation will keep the regressions out. It won't tell you the work is finished. A green build and a conformant site are different things, and WCAG 2.2 widened the gap between them.

The tests and both test pages are small enough to read in one sitting. Copy what's useful, change the selectors, and keep the broken page around: it's a handy way to check that a test fails when it should.

About the author: [name] builds WCAGsafe, an accessibility scanner. The study data above comes from its scans; the code in this article is independent of it and runs against any page.

#10263. Samsung fans continue to hate on Galaxy S27 Ultra redesign

An alleged new video shows Galaxy S27 dummy units that look like the latest leaks, and Samsung fans aren’t having it.

At this point, it’s anyone’s best guess as to what the Galaxy S27 series looks like, but it’s seeming surer and surer that Samsung will be giving the series a big redesign this coming year. Chris Franklin, a writer at SammyGuru, posted an unboxing video of the Galaxy S27 on X, citing that there’s a possibility the unboxing is AI-created — there are some eyebrow-raising reflections here, but it’s hard to tell for certain. The design looks similar to the “confirmed” renders that leaked a few days ago, although there are some subtle differences.

I don't think much about the design of phones, they're essentially all the same, especially when considering you're looking at the front 90% of the time, but I've always found the Galaxy's vertical camera layout to be the least attractive of any of the major phones. I think this looks much better.

Unsurprisingly, another leak from earlier in the month has a similar look but, again, with subtle differences. So far, all the major leaks in the past few months are pointing to Samsung outfitting the Galaxy S27 series with a raised camera island that looks extremely similar to Apple’s design on the iPhone 17 Pro and 18 Pro series.

Samsung isn’t alone here. The Xiaomi 18 series and Honor Magic 9 Pro series both have eerily similar designs, and many other 2026 and 2027 phones have followed suit. The most obvious problem with Samsung’s potential design (and, by extension, Apple’s) is the raised camera lenses, which protrude above the raised camera island. Xiaomi’s island is flat, following Google’s idea that a camera bar should protect the lenses and also keep the phone from wobbling or feeling off-balance.

Drama has already taken off on X (formerly Twitter), where Noah Cat reposted the video, and it seems Samsung fans aren’t happy. Samsung has needed to change up the vertical camera bar for years, as it has regularly gotten in the way of Qi2 and MagSafe magnetic accessories. This new design might help that, but it seems fans wanted Samsung to return to the Galaxy S10 horizontal camera bar days, not this new iPhone-esque raised island.

#10260. 35-year-old SNES runs ChatGPT, accelerated with a Super FX 2 chip — 21 MHz of RISC muscle speeds 2.2M parameter chatbot replies

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The project is available as a ROM that will run on real hardware with a flashcart system, or you can test it in an emulator or browser.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Nintendo’s classic SNES console now has its own ChatGPT chatbot. Due to the platform's age and limited resources, things are kept in check, though. For example, the (small) language model is a 2.2-million-parameter distillation of OpenAI’s GPT-OSS-120b. Nevertheless, Vintage Computing blogger Benj Edwards has attempted to squeeze the best performance from this incongruous tech combo by accelerating the chatbot's responsiveness using “the Super NES's best GPU, the Super FX 2 chip.”

AI runs on GPUs, so I wondered: how good would @ChatGPT be if it ran on the Super NES's best GPU, the Super FX 2 chip? With the help of GPT-6 Astra and gpt-oss, I present unofficial ChatGPT for SNES: 2.2M parameters on a real Super Nintendo. It can make mistakes, but it's fun https://t.co/TAJ4jYREo7November 19, 2026

The above Tweet shows you the SNES app splash screen, chat window, and an animation of someone interacting with chat. If that’s tempting you to give ChatGPT for SNES a test run, there are several options. Easiest first, you can head on over to this page where Edwards has embedded the SNES9x emulator, which can load the app. Once there, choose the 4MB model, or a 19MB one with the opening sentences of 150,000 Simple English Wikipedia articles integrated into the knowledge base (stored in the cartridge's MSU-1 data file). We recommend the latter, so the model knows a few more facts and can work as a calculator.

When you load it, the SNES app asks you to press Start. If you are sat at your PC keyboard, just hit Enter. You don’t need a joypad for text entry either, thankfully; just get typing to interact with ChatGPT for SNES (as long as the window is in focus). While you’re interacting with this chatbot, you can enjoy SNES-appropriate input sound effects, plus a chirpy chip-tune soundtrack.

In our tests, the usefulness of the ChatGPT for SNES app, beyond its curiosity value, was very limited. Edwards admits as much on the project's embedded interactive page, explaining that “the model is millions of times smaller than the models behind ChatGPT.” Naturally, with a small and fuzzy knowledge base, “You’ll get some fun and weird stuff,” says the Vintage Computing blogger.

Using Nintendo Super FX 2 technology to accelerate the language model is a masterstroke for the project. This coprocessor chip enhanced SNES games with faster 2D and 3D graphics and debuted with Yoshi's Island. Inside the SFX2, which had to be manufacturer-installed on any game cartridge that required it, was a 21.47 MHz RISC CPU. Contrast that to the SNES main CPU, a 16-bit Ricoh 5A22 running at 2.68 MHz.

Nintendo's second version of the SFX retained the polygon and advanced 2D effect capabilities of the original but ran at full speed internally (rather than half speed) and supported larger ROM sizes. Like Yoshi’s Island, it would be integrated into the Doom cartridge.

Edwards’ ChatGPT for SNES isn’t just an online diversion. It can also be downloaded and run/played on a real vintage SNES. You’ll also need a flash cart for the console with Super FX support, and MSU-1 support if you prefer the build including the abridged Wikipedia fact book.

#10258. Microsoft is shutting down the ‘To Do’ app that replaced Wunderlist, with no real replacement

Microsoft announced this week that its To Do app is shutting down soon, taking the legacy of Wunderlist with it.

Microsoft is shutting down its To Do app at the end of June 2027, with focus moving to the newer Planner app. The company explains:

As we bring personal task management into Planner, we will phase out To Do. The To Do apps will retire on June 30, 2027. Connections that other apps use to access To Do tasks will continue beyond that date, and we’ll provide separate notice before those connections are retired. We’re also updating existing task integrations across Microsoft 365 to connect with Planner, so you’ll be able to create or manage tasks in the Microsoft experiences you already use. Although To Do tasks are already visible in Planner, they will not be migrated to the new private plan. Customers using personal Microsoft accounts will need to choose another app. Our To Do transition guidance  will help you understand what’s changing and plan your next steps.

To Do was built on the foundation of Wunderlist, a wildly popular to do list app that Microsoft purchased in 2015. Wunderlist later shut down in 2020.

Microsoft’s shuffle, notably, leaves general consumers without a replacement app. To Do was available to anyone with a Microsoft account, where Planner is available only to enterprise/business users. So, for long-time Wunderlist users who followed Microsoft migration, this is the end of the road and it’s officially time to move to another app.

#10256. Hostinger VPS in 2026: Hands-On With the AI-Managed VPS

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A few years ago, renting a VPS meant signing up to be a part-time sysadmin. You’d SSH into a blank Ubuntu prompt and lose a Saturday to Nginx configs and firewall rules before writing a single line of your actual project.

In 2026, three things have changed. What we build has changed: AI coding tools turn weekend ideas into working apps, and AI agents need a machine that stays on after your laptop closes. Who runs servers has changed: you can now ask an AI agent in your terminal to set up a reverse proxy, close a port or work out why something broke. And what servers cost has changed: the AI boom is soaking up the world’s memory supply, and hosting prices are rising with it.

So I bought the smallest Hostinger VPS plan, deployed a Node.js app in Docker, and handed the DevOps work to Hostinger Agent, the AI assistant built into the dashboard.

Below is what that felt like step by step and whether it’s worth locking in a price before they climb further.

Most VPS hosting for developers now falls into four buckets. They all run on the same kind of machine; what changes is how much RAM you give them.

Apps built with AI coding tools. Claude Code and Cursor get you to a working Node.js or Python app fast, then leave you wondering where to put it. A VPS gives you one fixed monthly bill instead of usage-based pricing. If you want push-to-deploy, Hostinger offers Ubuntu templates with Coolify or Dokploy preinstalled.

AI agents and automations that run 24/7. For most people, a “VPS for AI” means a CPU box that never sleeps, not a GPU. Think n8n workflows, a scheduled scraper, or your own agent in a Docker container. Hostinger even has an “Ubuntu 24.04 with Claude Code” template: SSH in, run claude /login, and the agent keeps working after you close your laptop.

APIs, backends and databases for side projects. A Postgres instance, a Redis cache and a small API behind a reverse proxy fit comfortably on a mid-sized plan. You pick the versions, the extensions and the backup schedule.

Client sites and stores. Freelancers outgrow shared hosting when one client needs a custom PHP extension or a WooCommerce store starts to crawl. A VPS with cPanel or Plesk, or a WordPress or WooCommerce template, keeps client work isolated and under your control.

If you’d rather not build anything from scratch, the Docker Manager catalog listed 1,017 one-click templates when I checked, including 93 under AI/ML and 162 under Developer tools.

For this Hostinger VPS review, the question I cared about was simple: how long from “Choose plan” to an app on the internet? On KVM 1, the entry plan, it was about 12 minutes. Here’s each step with rough timings.

I went with KVM 1: 1 vCPU, 4 GB RAM, 50 GB NVMe storage and 4 TB of bandwidth. On the Black Friday deal it costs $6.49/mo, paid upfront as $155.76 for 24 months, and renews at $11.99/mo.

You choose the server’s location and what to install during checkout, alongside your billing details. The location picker shows estimated latency for each data centre; mine showed about 80 ms.

Install options come in three tabs: Plain OS, Control panel and Applications. I picked plain Ubuntu 26.04 LTS to see how much work the AI would take off my hands. A daily backup add-on is offered, but weekly backups are already included.

The five-minute estimate was about right. The “you’re ready” screen then gives you two ways in: the VPS dashboard, or a pre-filled ssh root@ command. It also offers a free domain for a year to use as the server’s hostname.

The overview shows the OS, plan, CPU, RAM, disk and bandwidth at a glance, with Docker Manager, Backups & Monitoring, Security and DNS Manager in the side menu.

Every server also gets a hostname under hstgr.cloud (mine was srvXXXXXXX.hstgr.cloud), which came in handy later for SSL. I’ve masked my hostname as srvXXXXXXX throughout and blurred IP addresses in the screenshots.

Plain Ubuntu doesn’t ship with Docker. Open Docker Manager in hPanel and it installs Docker for you with one click. From there you can deploy apps from a Compose file, a repository URL or the one-click catalog.

Click Web console and you’re logged in as root in a browser tab, with Hostinger Agent in a panel beside it. You don’t need an SSH client or keys. The login banner flagged 33 pending updates, 23 of them security fixes; more on that later. A quick docker --version confirmed Docker 29.9.3 was ready.

Here’s the whole app: a tiny HTTP server with a health check, and a Dockerfile on Node.js 24, a supported LTS line.

The build took 8.3 seconds, including pulling node:24-alpine, and the health check answered straight away. The --restart unless-stopped flag brings the container back after a crash or reboot, which I tested later.

That’s a working app, but on plain HTTP, on a high port, with no TLS. Getting it production-ready is the DevOps part I handed to the AI.

An AI-managed VPS isn’t the same as a traditional managed VPS. You still get a self-managed server with full root access, but Hostinger Agent does the work you’d normally do yourself. Agent (previously called Kodee) is an MCP-powered assistant that lives in the dashboard and beside the AI web terminal, and it’s included at no extra cost. You can also connect Claude or Cursor to your server through Hostinger’s MCP server.

My prompt: “Check that Docker is installed, then set up a reverse proxy with a free SSL certificate so my demo-app container (port 3000) is served at https://srvXXXXXXX.hstgr.cloud.”

It started by looking around: the Docker and Compose versions, which ports were in use, whether a web server was already running, and the OS release. Then it installed Caddy from Caddy’s official repository, configured the proxy and made sure ufw allowed web traffic.

It read Caddy’s logs to confirm the Let’s Encrypt certificate, which arrived in about 6 seconds. Finally it tested its own work: HTTPS returned 200, and plain HTTP returned a 308 redirect. The whole task took about two minutes.

To give it a real fault, I ran docker stop demo-app and reloaded the site. Then I asked why it was returning a 502 and to fix it.

In a single command, it checked the container state (Exited (137)) and searched Caddy’s logs, where it found connect: connection refused on port 3000. It restarted the container, confirmed /health and HTTPS were back, and even pointed to my docker stop in the terminal history. It also explained that --restart unless-stopped covers crashes and reboots, but not a manual stop.

A tip it didn’t mention: exit code 137 means Docker had to force-kill the container, because Node running as PID 1 ignores SIGTERM. Add --init to docker run for clean, instant shutdowns.

This was the most impressive answer. ufw already allowed only those three ports, but Agent noticed port 3000 was still reachable from the internet, because Docker-published ports bypass ufw rules.

It recreated the container bound to 127.0.0.1:3000, so the app is only reachable through Caddy, and confirmed with an external port check. It left DNS, time sync and Caddy’s local admin port running, explaining that stopping them would break DNS and time sync.

Then I asked it to reboot the server. First it checked that Docker and Caddy start on boot and that the container’s restart policy was set, then it rebooted. The site came back over HTTPS without any help from me.

Nothing I asked for was destructive, so Agent just got on with it: the firewall change, the container rebuild and the reboot all ran without a confirmation prompt.

The Agent panel says so upfront, noting that terminal commands can run without one. For destructive actions such as reinstalling the OS or restoring a backup, Hostinger’s FAQ says Agent always asks for confirmation first.

One thing worth knowing: to close port 3000, Agent removed and recreated my container. That was harmless for a stateless demo, but a container that keeps data without a volume would lose it. Take a snapshot before big jobs, and keep the panel in view.

Here are the four plans at their Black Friday rates for a 24-month term.

For scale, here’s what my test used. With the Node app, Docker and Caddy all running, KVM 1 had 3.3 GB of its 4 GB RAM still available, and the app container itself used about 65 MB.

So 4 GB is plenty for one or two small apps. Move to 8 GB on KVM 2 once you add Coolify, a database, or n8n with its own Postgres, because those are the services that eat memory.

Finding a cheap VPS got harder in 2026, because memory got expensive. AI data centres are buying up DRAM, and hosts are passing the cost on. In April 2026, Hetzner raised cloud server prices by up to 37%, citing hardware costs, then raised prices again in June. On the same April date, OVHcloud’s entry VPS went from $4.90 to $7.60, a 55% jump. Nobody can promise where VPS prices go next, but the trend this year has been up.

This is how Hostinger’s Black Friday pricing works. You pay upfront for 12 or 24 months, and the 24-month term gives the lowest monthly rate. Your price is fixed for that whole term. After that, it renews at the standard rate.

On KVM 2, for example, you pay $210.96 today (the $8.79/mo rate) for 24 months, and it renews at $14.99/mo after that. Do the maths on the renewal before you commit, not just the headline rate.

Black Friday VPS deal: up to 70% off VPS hosting

Get up to 70% off Hostinger VPS plans this Black Friday, with prices from $6.49/mo on a 24-month term. Every plan includes Hostinger Agent, Docker Manager and free weekly backups. Your price is fixed for the term, then renews at the standard rate. The deal ends December 14, 2026.

Rapidus may miss its semiconductor mass production deadline in 2027.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Rapidus is reportedly facing challenges in developing functioning SRAM cells using its 2nm-class node, which relies on EUV lithography. According to a new report, Rapidus has missed a critical milestone to demonstrate working SRAM cells this summer, despite the Japanese company's efforts in producing semiconductor test structures at its fab in Japan in mid-2025. In addition, the company is allegedly fighting contamination in its cleanroom. This puts the plans of the government-backed startup to begin mass production of chips in 2027 at risk.

Bloomberg's report spells bad news for the Japanese firm. Rapidus began to produce test structures with 2nm-class Gate-All-Around (GAA) transistors at its IIM-1 facility in Hokkaido in July 2025, nearly 16 months ago. At the time, the company announced that its prototype transistors exhibited the expected electrical characteristics, which suggested that its equipment and fundamental process steps worked as intended.

"Then we had a testing and July 10th we confirmed a major milestone: we successfully obtained electrical characteristics of 2-nanometer node GAA transistors," said Atsuyoshi Koikem, chief executive of Rapidus, at Hot Chips 2025. "We can see the transistor actually works."

However, production of individual transistors is considerably easier than fabrication of functional integrated circuits (ICs) with multiple interconnected devices. According to sources at Bloomberg, Rapidus was supposed to demonstrate functional static random-access memory (SRAM) cells by summer 2026, but has yet to achieve this milestone. It is noteworthy that the report does not reveal whether Rapidus is struggling with individual SRAM cells, larger memory arrays, or achieving acceptable electrical characteristics and yields, so we will have to speculate.

SRAM is fundamental to modern processors because it serves as cache memory and forms other on-chip memory structures, which can contain hundreds of millions of bitcells. Furthermore, SRAM arrays are important vehicles for early process evaluation, as their repetitive structures expose manufacturing defects, transistor variability, and other problems that may not become apparent during tests of individual transistors.

A conventional 6T SRAM bitcell consists of six transistors: two cross-coupled inverters (four transistors) and two access transistors. However, these transistors also require contacts and local interconnects, as well as connections to wordlines, bitlines, and power rails (VDD and VSS). Meanwhile, a complete SRAM macro also requires peripheral circuits, such as row decoders, sense amplifiers, write drivers, and control logic, which makes a 256 KB SRAM macro quite a sophisticated IC. As a result, chipmakers usually use both SRAM cell arrays and SRAM macros as primary means to evaluate, monitor, and qualify process technologies at various stages. Furthermore, low defect density of SRAM macros is crucial for good yields of SRAM-intensive chip designs.

The importance of SRAM makes Rapidus' reported difficulties particularly concerning given its intention to begin commercial production in 2027. While the company does not confirm delays in mass production using its 2nm fabrication technology, in an interview with the Japan Times, representatives at Rapidus already said that the company was on track to mass-produce chips in the second half of fiscal 2027, which ends on March 31, 2028. Previously, Rapidus had specified its production targets without specifying calendar years or fiscal years, which by default implied the calendar year.

Before it can mass-produce processors for customers, the company must demonstrate functional SRAM cells, integrate them into larger arrays, achieve acceptable yields, qualify its process technology, and then initiate risk production around a year before the start of high-volume manufacturing (HVM).

Chipmakers do not generally disclose detailed schedules for SRAM development and milestones as far as new manufacturing technologies are concerned. However, TSMC tends to share progress on its upcoming nodes using the performance and yield of a 256 Mb SRAM array or '256 Mb SRAM yield on an internal product-like vehicle.' For example, TSMC's N2 demonstrated more than 80% of its target device performance and over 50% yield on a 256Mb SRAM test chip in April 2023. By April 2024, the process had advanced to more than 90% of its target device performance and over 80% SRAM yield. About half a year before mass production started, in May 2025, N2 transistor performance was close to 100%, whereas 256 Mb SRAM average yield was over 90%. By contrast, Rapidus has not announced any SRAM progress so far, and Bloomberg reports that the company has failed to produce functioning static random access memory cells, meaning that its SRAM yield is 0%.

While there is no rule of thumb that a company must have a functional SRAM array or macro N quarters before mass production of chips with that array or macro, it is evident that the reported setback leaves Rapidus with limited time to complete its crucial development milestones before kicking off mass production by the end of fiscal 2027.

Production of 2nm-class chips with GAA transistors and extreme ultraviolet lithography is exceptionally complicated, even for established chipmakers. So far, only Intel, TSMC, and Samsung have begun mass production of chips using nodes that rely on GAA devices. Unlike FinFETs, GAA transistors feature multiple vertically stacked silicon nanosheets surrounded by gate materials, which improves electrostatic control but introduces additional fabrication challenges. Furthermore, extremely small feature dimensions require exceptional precision in deposition, etching, lithography, and process control. For Rapidus, which was founded only in 2022 and relies on process technology IP developed by IBM, the task of reproducing these processes consistently in a new fab represents an incredibly tall task.

One potential problem involves selective removal of silicon-germanium (SiGe) layers that separate silicon nanosheets during fabrication. These sacrificial layers must be removed without damage to the silicon channels or significant changes to their dimensions. Insufficient etching can leave unwanted material between nanosheets, whereas excessive etching can reduce channel thickness or increase surface roughness. A research paper published by imec identifies nanosheet release as one of the critical challenges of GAA manufacturing, particularly because variations in channel geometry can affect transistor performance and consistency.

In fact, EUV lithography itself introduces additional complications. At extremely small dimensions, stochastic effects associated with photon absorption, resist chemistry, and subsequent pattern transfer can produce variations in feature dimensions, edge roughness, and occasional missing or malformed structures. Such defects can affect pretty much everything, including transistor gates, contacts, and interconnects. In fact, there is a company called Fractilia that specializes solely in stochastic controls. Although individual test transistors may function correctly, circuits with millions of interconnected devices are much more vulnerable to failures caused by stochastic defects.

Another potential challenge concerns inner spacer formation. These extremely small dielectric structures electrically isolate the gate from the source and drain regions. Their fabrication requires precise lateral etching of SiGe followed by dielectric deposition. Variations in spacer geometry can affect effective gate length, parasitic capacitance, leakage current, and transistor performance. A paper published in Nanomaterials in January 2026 demonstrates that even subtle differences in etching conditions can affect spacer geometry and nanosheet integrity, which potentially introduces electrical variability that becomes particularly problematic in SRAM circuits.

Gate stack integration presents another possible source of difficulties. GAA fabrication requires conformal deposition of gate dielectrics and metal materials around multiple nanosheets, including narrow spaces between them. Variations in material thickness, interfaces, and effective metal work function can alter transistor threshold voltages, which is not a big problem for individual transistors or tiny test patterns. However, for SRAM structures, excessive differences between nominally identical transistors can reduce read, write, and retention margins, which could prevent cells from operating reliably at their intended voltages and greatly narrow the manufacturing process window.

Finally, contact resistance, interconnect defects, and particle contamination can further complicate circuit integration. Bloomberg specifically reports that Rapidus is working to improve dust and particle control in its cleanroom. Normally, there might be no connection between contamination and the SRAM woes the company is experiencing. However, one of the distinct features of IIM-1 is that it uses an unconventional grid-based wafer transportation system to enable its single-batch processing as well as increase the efficiency of expedited processing. Usage of this system raises questions about whether the new infrastructure has undergone sufficient contamination and reliability qualification, though, again, a transportation system alone could not be responsible for zero (or near-zero) SRAM yield.

Rapidus reportedly stresses that it remains committed to mass production in the second half of fiscal 2027. However, until the company demonstrates functional SRAM and subsequently larger integrated circuits, questions about its ability to manufacture commercially viable 2nm chips will persist.

Anton Shilov is a contributing writer at Tom’s Hardware. Over the past couple of decades, he has covered everything from CPUs and GPUs to supercomputers and from modern process technologies and latest fab tools to high-tech industry trends.

#10250. Expect Samsung’s good-value Galaxy devices to get more expensive, report says

Rising memory costs are hitting mid-range devices the hardest. The latest report suggests Samsung will increase the price of its Galaxy A device series by 19% next year.

The report comes from Korean news outlet SEDaily. An industry source has reportedly stated that Samsung plans to increase the price of its mid-range phones starting next year. More specifically, the Galaxy A series.

The range between price jumps varies across a reported 8 Galaxy A series devices, but the highest value difference nearly hits a notable 19% increase. The largest jump reaches 18.6% for the Galaxy A17, which could increase from $269 to $319. It would no longer sit in a sub-$300 spot and wouldn’t be much of a value buy anymore.

Again, these are phones praised for sitting at a convenient price point. Samsung’s Galaxy A devices are generally good value and offer a lot of the best One UI features for a price that doesn’t come close to the current flagship selling points.

This comes after a report came out last month indicating Samsung cut production of its Galaxy A devices by “approximately 20%.” Memory makes up more of the overall component cost in mid-range devices. So the cost of the overall device is more influenced by memory prices than in a flagship, like the Galaxy S26 Ultra. Even with a specced-out build, there’s more dispersion of costly components, rather than one expensive component like memory taking up the bulk of the production price.

That cut is estimated to be around 10 million units, and this latest report only furthers the claim that mid-range devices are too costly to make for Samsung.

The opposite is true for the Galaxy S27 Pro and Ultra, which reportedly got the green light for increased production prior to launch. Price suggestions average an increase of $200 for the Galaxy S27 and Galaxy S27 Plus. As for the high-spec Galaxy S27 Pro and Ultra, they start at $1,399 and $1,499, respectively.

There was already a massive disparity between those devices and the increasingly validated Galaxy A devices, and the gap is shrinking.

#10247. Modder cools gaming laptop with a bounce house blower for a 35-degree drop

Trash Bench is cooling laptops again, now using a bounce house blower fan and reaching temperatures below 50 degrees Celsius.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Experimental PC modder Trash Bench recently tested a laptop cooler and tried to beat it with increasingly powerful blower setups, eventually opting for a bounce house inflator blower to cool the i7-10750H and RTX 2070 MaxQ with a 90W power limit. He was able to bring temperatures down from 84 degrees Celsius to 48.8 degrees Celsius, with Cyberpunk going from 47 FPS to 56 FPS.

Trash Bench was asked to review a laptop cooling stand, but he said he doesn’t do reviews, so he proposed a video idea: He’ll test the laptop cooler and try to beat it with his creations—no opening the laptop, no sub-ambient cooling solutions, only airflow.

The Llano V12 laptop cooling fan sent to Trash Bench achieved an impressive 58.3 degrees Celsius average on the GPU at medium settings, dropping 27 degrees from the stock 84 degrees Celsius. The lower temperatures also improved gaming performance by an average of 19.4%. Pushing the Llano V12 to 100% made it incredibly loud, but it only reached 56.7 degrees Celsius with negligible performance gains.

His first attempt to beat the Llano V12 was using the Noctua NF-A20, a huge 200mm fan, and a 3D-printed duct with foam around the edges, like the Llano V12, to prevent air leaks. Unfortunately, the single 200mm fan didn't beat the Llano V12, with GPU temperatures averaging 66.3 degrees Celsius—still pretty impressive given the setup's simplicity, if you ignore the messy PC power supply and fan speed controller beside it.

His second attempt used six Arctic P12 fans at full speed, beating the Llano V12 by 0.9 degrees Celsius at medium settings and achieving an average GPU temperature of 57.4 degrees Celsius. However, it lost to the full-speed Llano V12, but only barely. The third attempt used two 4000 RPM server fans, which were even louder than the six full-speed Arctic P12S — but that finally did the trick, beating the Llano V12 at 100% by 2.6 degrees Celsius.

And just to go extreme, like Trash Bench often does in his experiments, he used a powerful blower fan designed to inflate bounce houses. He took advantage of the larger foam gaskets the Llano V12 used and 3D-printed an interface at the bottom of the laptop cooler’s housing to fit a flexible duct. The sheer force of the air lifted the laptop off the stand, so it needed to be taped down to seal the air leak.

The bounce house blower produced an average GPU temperature of 48.8 degrees Celsius, but saw very little performance gain over the Llano V12 at 100%, six Arctic P12s, and the pair of server fans, with Time Spy benchmark results within 1% of each other. The Noctua was the weakest in the benchmarks, however, lagging by 3% behind the bounce house blower, but was much quieter.

Trash Bench admits that while lower temperatures are great, they reach diminishing returns once the thermal bottleneck disappears and another bottleneck takes over.

#10244. Is Kling 4.0 the Right AI Video Generator for Your Project?

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

A short clip can help a project in different ways. It can show an idea you have not filmed, put an existing design in motion, or give an announcement something to look at. The same approach will not work if the clip needs to demonstrate a real feature or show exactly what happened. Before choosing a generator, decide what the viewer needs to learn from the shot.

This article uses Kling 4.0 as a worked example, but the decision process applies to any AI video generator. It covers how these tools turn prompts and images into video, how Kling 4.0 and a few alternatives differ, how to choose a starting mode, how to add exact text and branding outside the generated clip with a short ffmpeg script, and when real footage is the better choice. Feature details come from each vendor’s own pages at the time of writing and change often, so confirm current terms before you plan a production.

● Generated video is best for imagined shots. It suits concepts, backgrounds and early explorations, not instructions or faithful records.

● Your starting material decides the mode. A written idea, a reference image or a pair of opening and closing frames each call for a different input.

● Keep exact wording out of the generated clip. Titles, dates and logos belong on separate layers in an editor.

● Tools differ on control, cost and licensing. Compare inputs, length, resolution, credits and terms before you commit.

● Always review. Watch the whole clip at its final size and check it against the real product or design.

Most current systems extend diffusion models across time. A model learns to turn noise into a coherent set of frames guided by a prompt, and sometimes by an image or a clip. The research paper Video Diffusion Models describes this extension from images to video. The practical consequences are easy to observe.

● The prompt describes change over time. One visible change and one camera instruction usually work better than a long list of actions.

● Reference material steers but does not lock. A picture can suggest shape and colour without guaranteeing an exact match.

● Longer clips give more room for drift. Faces, logos and fine text are the first things to change between frames.

● Cost grows with length and resolution. Most hosted tools charge credits per second, so short previews are cheaper than full shots.

If you only have an idea, start with text. For a short announcement, you might ask for a band of light to move across a wall of coloured tiles. No one needs to recognise a particular wall, so the model can decide how it looks. Give it one visible change and a clear view of the subject. A prompt such as Soft morning light travels slowly across a wall of blue ceramic tiles. Fixed camera, no lettering is enough to see whether the idea works.

If you already have an illustration or a photo, reference mode gives you another starting point. Suppose you want a short concept clip for a desk accessory that only exists as a drawing. The picture can show the general shape and colour while the prompt describes the view you want. A video reference can help when it is the motion that is hard to explain, such as a slow camera slide. Say what each asset is meant to guide, and do not add a clip just because you have one.

Use keyframes when a particular view matters at a particular moment. Start with an opening image. If a short reveal also needs to end with the object facing forward, add an ending image to show that view. That is different from a reference image that mainly shows what the object looks like. Neither method guarantees an exact match, so check the result rather than assuming the uploaded picture has locked the design.

No single tool fits every project. The table summarizes what each option’s own page states, and “not stated” means the page did not say, not that the feature is absent.

Reading the table: hosted studios such as the one on JXP are the quickest way to test an idea and need no setup, but you pay per second and work within their supported inputs. Kling’s own site is the place to check announcements and API options directly, since its page says little about durations or resolution. Runway positions its models for motion quality and prompt following, while Google’s Gemini API video documentation suits teams building generation into their own products. The Diffusers video guide covers open-source pipelines for developers who want local control and are ready to manage GPUs and licences.

Trying an idea before making it. An illustrator might want to see how an imagined landscape feels when the light changes. A quick clip gives the team something to discuss: is the change visible, and does it happen at the right pace? It does not need to be the final animation to answer those questions.

Showing a product concept. A rotating view or a slow camera move can help people discuss how a design might be presented. It is useful early, when the shape and lighting are still being explored. Once the clip represents a product someone can buy, the standard changes. If a port moves or a button appears that the real product does not have, it is the wrong clip for that job.

Making a short visual for a post. A few seconds of moving colour can give an event announcement or a project update a distinctive opening. Put the name, date and other exact wording in your editor so they are readable and easy to change. The background only needs to support the message. If it competes with the text, use less movement or keep the background still.

Generated video is unreliable at lettering, so add titles in an editor or with a script. The command below overlays a title on a generated background and then checks the file properties. It assumes you already have a clip named background.mp4 and ffmpeg installed.

-vf "drawtext=text='Launch Night | 12 March':fontcolor=white:fontsize=64:x=(w-text_w)/2:y=(h-text_h)/2:box=1:boxcolor=black@0.35:boxborderw=24" \

-c:v libx264 -pix_fmt yuv420p -an announcement.mp4

-show_entries stream=width,height,r_frame_rate,duration \

-of default=noprint_wrappers=1 announcement.mp4

Because the text lives in the command, you can reuse the same background for three events by changing one string. The font may need to be set with a fontfile option on some systems. This is the practical version of keeping the moving background in one file and the editable details in another.

If people will follow the clip as instructions, use real footage or a screen recording for the steps. A convincing shot of an app is not proof that a button is in the right place. A generated close-up of a product is not a reliable way to show how its parts fit. You can still use an imagined opening, but make the switch to the real demonstration clear.

Think about revisions, too. If your team expects to adjust the same title, logo or date every week, put those elements on separate layers in an editor. And if the only movement you need is a slow zoom on an approved picture, apply that zoom directly. There is no reason to risk changing the picture when a simple edit will do.

This matters when someone else will maintain the video. A reusable announcement template is easier to hand over if the moving background is one file and the event details are editable. If the team likes the movement but needs three versions with different dates, they can reuse the same clip. If every change calls for a new generation, the time you saved on the first version may disappear on the second.

The Kling 4.0 video generator on JXP currently offers 720p and 1080p settings and clip lengths from 3 to 30 seconds. According to its page, reference mode accepts up to 10 images and 5 videos, keyframe mode takes a first frame, middle frames and an optional last frame, and credits are charged per second with a higher rate at 1080p. For a first attempt, choose the frame shape you plan to use and make the clip only as long as it needs to be. Check the displayed credit cost before generating, because a short preview can tell you whether the direction works before you spend on a longer shot.

Decide what a good result looks like before you generate it. For the tile background, you need to see the light move without distracting from the words you will add. For a product concept, check whether the shape stays recognisable as the viewpoint changes. Watch the whole clip at the size the audience will see it. If the shot misses the point, revise one thing at a time: the motion, the framing or the source image.

● Read the licence. JXP’s page lists a commercial use license on each plan and says outputs are for creative and lawful use, with the user responsible for reviewing them. Other tools have different terms.

● Use inputs you have the right to use. Do not upload other people’s copyrighted work or photos of people without permission.

● Do not misrepresent products or events. A generated clip should not suggest features or footage that do not exist.

● Consider provenance. C2PA is an open standard for Content Credentials that records where content came from and how it was edited, and some tools support it.

● Disclose where required. Follow platform and advertising rules on labelling AI-generated or altered content.

● Fine text, logos, hands and fast motion can drift or flicker between frames.

● Reference images and keyframes steer the result but do not guarantee an exact match.

● Vendor specifications, such as resolution and length, can differ between a model announcement and a particular platform.

● Credit costs and plan terms change, so check them on the day you generate.

● Long or exact sequences are often cheaper and safer to film than to generate.

Short imagined shots, concept clips and moving backgrounds, where some details can be left open and the clip is reviewed before use.

Use text when you only have an idea, reference mode when you have a picture or clip to guide the look, and keyframes when specific views matter at specific moments.

Not reliably. Use real footage or a screen recording for instructions and demonstrations.

No. Hosted studios, vendor sites, developer APIs and open-source pipelines all exist. Compare supported inputs, length, resolution, cost and licensing against your project.

Kling 4.0 is a good candidate when the missing piece is an imagined shot and you can describe what it needs to do. Pick one such shot, try it, and put it next to the material you already have, and compare at least one alternative before you commit. If the project needs exact instructions or a faithful record, use the real thing. A useful first result is one that does its job in the edit, not simply one that looks impressive on its own.

Get the freshest news and resources for developers, designers and digital creators in your inbox each week

Something to note – Google Pixel is technically growing this year compared to last. Just out of view on this latest update, Q2 2026 saw Google’s share up 1% year-over-year, and Q1 was also up compared to the same period in 2025. #10241. The two best kids’ smartwatches are $50-$64 off ahead of Black Friday

Garmin and Bark are competing for your money this year, and these sales make it easy to get something fun that’ll keep your kids safe and in touch.

Many parents struggle with the idea of getting a smartphone for their kids. If you want your kids to stay in touch but not have access to all the garbage on the internet, a smartwatch is the best alternative. I’ve tested a lot of kids’ smartwatches over the years, and I can easily recommend the two in the picture above: the Bark Watch and the Garmin Bounce 2.

Both watches are on sale well ahead of Black Friday, too! The Bark Watch is $11 off at Amazon ($274 total) and $50 off at Bark, while the Garmin Bounce 2 is $50-$64 off (starting at $235) depending on the color you choose. The least expensive Garmin Bounce 2 is the blue color in the photo I took above, which my son loved.

Choosing between the two is easy enough. Garmin’s watch is the least complicated and comes in a slightly more durable case with fun colors. It’s slightly cheaper than Bark, but doesn’t have any health sensors on board. It mainly tracks steps, allows your kids to message or call you, and gives you geofencing and location tracking through a $9.99 per month LTE subscription plan. It’s got a 5ATM rating and a polycarbonate case, so it’s quite durable.

The Garmin Bounce 2 is preloaded with a few mini games and educational apps, plus the Garmin Jr. app has built-in unlockables and rewards parents can set for doing chores, completing schoolwork, etc. Battery life is roughly 2 days with normal use, but can be less if you’re in an area with poor LTE reception. The Garmin Bounce 2 is best for kids 10 and under.

The Bark Watch uses Samsung Galaxy Watch 8 hardware and a custom version of Wear OS. It’s a much more powerful watch and only gets a day of battery life because of it. This one feels like a midpoint between a kids’ watch and a standard Wear OS watch, as it has all the power of Wear OS but doesn’t feature the Google Play Store. It doesn’t have any games, social media, or browsers.

Since it uses Samsung’s hardware, it includes cutting-edge health sensors that your kids can use, plus great multitasking and a svelte, attractive design. Bark charges $15 per month for its plan, which includes a Bark App subscription for the whole family. I also use the Bark App on my son’s phone, which helps me monitor his usage and make sure he’s safe online. The extra $5 per month adds up, but it’s absolutely worth it if you want to add the Bark App to another phone or tablet.

The Bark Watch is fine enough for younger kids, but the design and lack of games feel more like an “older kids’ watch” to me, and my 12-year-old son agrees. Either way you go, these are fantastic choices that you’ll be happy with, especially with a nice little deal.

#10240. Announcing Long-Context Training on TPUs with MaxText Context Parallelism

Long-context training — including pretraining and post-training, has become a foundation of frontier AI models. Longer native sequence lengths give large language models (LLMs) the working memory to read a whole code repository or ingest hours of video, and reason through multi-step agentic tasks. In three years, context windows have grown from 128K–200K tokens to 1M–2M, and models trained toward 10M tokens are arriving. Training at those lengths, not just serving them, is where the system limits show up.

In particular, efficient training at multi-million-token sequence lengths runs into the following bottlenecks:

Today, we are excited to announce long-context training support in MaxText, Google Cloud's open-source JAX training framework. MaxText enables this by supporting four context-parallel strategies on Cloud TPU: All-gather, A2A (DeepSpeed Ulysses), P2P (Ring Attention), and the A2A + P2P hybrid (Unified Sequence Parallelism, or USP), combined with load balancing and sequence packing. All four use the Tokamax Splash Attention kernel. Combined with the 3D inter-chip interconnect (ICI) ( supported on TPUs such as TPU 5p or TPU 7 (Ironwood) ), this lets one sequence span an entire slice while communication overlaps compute and memory per chip stays flat. In our benchmarks, Llama 3.1 8B (25 head dim, 16 query heads) demonstrates exceptional long-context efficiency, achieving 70% Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU) on TPU 5p for sequence lengths up to 4 million tokens. Across both TPU 5p and TPU 7, context can be seamlessly extended to 14 million tokens—with TPU 7 consistently maintaining 50% MFU across all sequence scales below 6 million tokens.

This functionality is available today in MaxText v0.2.4(doc).

Context parallelism splits a long sequence across chips, so no chip holds the activations of the whole sequence. Context parallelism can be combined with data and tensor parallelism, and the smallest context-parallel size that fits your sequence in HBM is usually the right one. This also holds for sparser attention variants which cut attention compute but not the activations of every other layer.

Attention is the one global operation where every token needs the keys and values of every preceding token, so each context parallel strategy has to bring those keys and values back together. Each makes a different tradeoff between memory (how much KV a chip holds), communication (how much moves per layer, and whether it overlaps compute), and model shape (how many attention heads to split).

All-gather: Each chip keeps its chunk of the sequence and, at every layer, gathers the keys and values from every other chip. It is the simplest option. However, because causal masking causes later tokens to attend to larger prefixes, All-gather requires balancing compute load across devices.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

All-to-All (Ulysses): Gathering keys and values makes every chip hold the whole sequence, so attention memory never shrinks no matter how many chips you add. All-to-All changes what each chip owns instead. Attention is independent across heads but not across tokens, so the head axis is free to shard and the token axis is not. An All-to-All before attention rotates the sharding from one onto the other: each chip gives up the heads it is not responsible for and receives the tokens it was missing for the heads it keeps, ending up with the full sequence for a few heads instead of a few tokens for all heads. This is also why Ulysses does not need token load balancing. Attention then runs as ordinary single-chip attention with standard kernels, and a second All-to-All rotates the output back. KV per chip shrinks by the context-parallel factor, but you cannot split across more chips than the model has heads — and tensor parallelism shards that same dimension, so TP × CP is capped by the head count (under GQA, by the KV heads: eight for Llama 3 8B). The all-to-all itself, though, does not overlap with compute — attention cannot start on a partial sequence, so every chip waits for data from every other chip before any of them begins.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

P2P (ring attention): Ulysses' ceiling is the head count; ring attention removes it by never leaving the token axis. Keys and values never materialize in full. KV chunks travel around a ring of chips one hop at a time, neighbor to neighbor. Each chip attends its queries to the chunk it holds while the next one is in flight, and merges the partial results as it goes. A chip holds its own chunk and one visiting chunk, never the full sequence, and the communication overlaps compute. However, dividing attention computation into smaller chunks can reduce local kernel efficiency, and unlike All-to-All Ulysses, ring attention requires explicit load balancing.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

A2A + P2P (USP): A2A + P2P (USP): Neither strategy scales on its own — All-to-All stops at the head count, and a long ring accumulates hops while thinning the overlap that makes it work. Unified sequence parallelism composes them on two axes of the chip mesh, so the context-parallel degree becomes the product of the two: All-to-All along one axis, sized at or below the KV-head count, and a ring along the other to extend past it. Because each All-to-All group has already pooled its tokens, a ring step now carries a chunk larger by the All-to-All factor, and the ring is correspondingly shorter — more compute per hop to hide the transfer behind, and fewer hops to accumulate latency and causal imbalance. On Cloud TPU the two map onto two axes of the slice's chip mesh.

Sorry, your browser doesn't support playback for this video

These strategies cover the attention variants in today's major models, from multi-head and grouped-query attention to DeepSeek-style multi-head latent attention.

Splitting the sequence only pays off if the attention compute on each chip stays efficient. All four strategies run on Splash Attention, the block-sparse flash attention kernel from Tokamax, Google's open-source library of TPU kernels. It skips fully masked blocks and handles packed sequences without padding. P2P calls it once per ring step, on the local queries and the KV chunk that has just arrived, while the transfer for the next step runs underneath, in the forward and backward pass alike. Each chip does the same fused, block-level work it would do without context parallelism, with the communication hidden behind it.

P2P (Ring Attention) is the primary workhorse for very long-context training, whereas All-Gather is the standard baseline for most other cases where the KV can fit. These are the primary CP strategies to use in practice. We compare how far each can push context length on a fixed 64-chip TPU 5p slice with Llama 3.1 8B (256 head dim, 16 query heads) , and measure their MFUs. In ideal settings where both strategies can have large enough chunks (S/CP) and local batch size - the two major variables driving MFU on TPU 5p, we observe that P2P can outperform All-Gather almost every context length from 64K to 14M tokens, where communication becomes mostly hidden with the Ring circular transport schedule.

TPU v7x (Ironwood) takes the same recipe further. With twice the HBM per chip, P2P training of 14M tokens on 64 TPU 7 chips reached 48.7% Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU) compared to 37.1% on TPU 5p. We observed that ring attention performs better than other context parallelism strategies -- e.g. Ulysses and USP –- on TPU 7 chips.

The choice of context parallelism strategy depends mostly on whether the gathered KV fits in HBM, and on how many KV heads the model has:

For all CP strategies, when you run low on memory: Offload before you recompute. MaxText can move saved activations, optimizer states, and parameters to host memory tensor by tensor. At long context lengths, these transfers are hidden behind compute, making offloading effectively free. Offload attention outputs first, followed by optimizer states, layer inputs, parameters, and finally remat as a last resort. MFU stays flat through the offload steps while all-gather falls off and eventually stops fitting. For more details, see the remat tuning guide.

Looking ahead, we see two natural extensions. The first is broader model coverage. Models that mix sliding-window and global layers, such as Gemma, would run the window layers with a short exchange between neighboring chips while the global layers use the ring. We also plan to support linear attention mechanisms such as Gated Delta Net (GDN) and Kimi Delta Attention (KDA). The second is performance, with further tuning of the Tokamax kernels and the P2P backward pass to narrow the remaining gap to all-gather at short context, and recipes by model and context length for TPU 5p and 7 chips.

Context parallelism is available today in MaxText v0.2.4 on PyPI, the corresponding Docker images and on GitHub main. Sample recipes are available here for TPU 7 and TPU 5p. Pick a strategy with context_parallel_strategy (ring, ulysses, or usp), set the context-parallel size, and keep the rest of your run the same. The sharding guide has the flags and constraints for each strategy, and the release notes link to the pull requests behind this work. We can't wait to see what you train.

#10236. Hands-on: Sony’s Walkman-revival USB-C headphones are back in stock and worth a look

Sony’s iconic Walkman headphones are back in the USB-C MDR-3R and, after quickly selling out on launch day, they’ve now been restocked. And, after using them, I can see why they sold out so quickly.

The MDR-3R are Sony’s revival of the wired over-ear headphones that many used with a classic Walkman player. The retro style still looks great in 2026, but using an original pair is a bit of a headache in today’s age of headphone jack-less smartphones. So it was nice to see Sony give this a revival with USB-C.

While the style won’t be for everyone, I think these work brilliantly. The sound quality is excellent and having a pair of headphones that doesn’t fully enclose my ears is pretty refreshing. There’s no noise cancellation, but with open-ear earbuds becoming more popular, I actually really like these as a similar solution (though high volumes will definitely be heard by those around you). I’m not fully cut off from the world around me, but I’m also able to get good sound quality. Lows are the weakness here, with unimpressive bass drops, but the sound quality is crisp and clear and pretty neutral, and the open-ish style makes it feel like the room around me is filled with music.

The physical hardware is great. While a plastic and metal build is objectively not as comfy as a premium pair of ANC headphones, the extremely light weight helps MDR-3R become an afterthought. You simply forget you’re wearing them after a few minutes.

The earpads are swappable, with orange and blue options included in the box.

As for the USB-C cable, it’s fully plug-and-play. No app needed and it worked across every device I tested (including Pixel, which doesn’t work with all USB-C audio output). I do wish the cable was perhaps a few inches longer, but it’s long enough to wear the headphones with my phone in my front pocket. There’s a remote along the cable for play/pause and volume adjustments, though I think it’s a little too high on the cable.

For $59.99, these are a fun pair of USB-C headphones to have on hand. They sound good, work great, and don’t cost an arm and a leg. I can see why they sold out so quickly on launch day – in just 10 hours, Sony told me.

Sony MDR-3R are back in stock now and shipping in early December. If you want a pair, I say don’t wait. They’re well worth at least trying if you can get them for retail price – please don’t give in to the scalpers selling these at double, they’re not worth that.

#10235. How Consumer AI Companions Handle Context Windows, Persistent Memory, and Voice Latency

Stay up to date with AI tools, model releases, and developer workflows that matter.

AI companions are the most demanding consumer use of large language models right now. A coding assistant can forget your last message and still be useful. A companion that forgets your name, your running story, or the plan you made last Tuesday has failed at its one job. On top of that, users now expect voice calls that feel like a phone conversation, not a walkie-talkie exchange with a five-second pause.

Three engineering problems decide whether a companion app works: how much of the conversation fits in the model's context window, what the app remembers once the window fills up, and how fast the voice pipeline answers. Here is how consumer companions handle each one, with real tier and pricing examples, and what developers can take from their designs for their own products.

Most LLM products are judged on single answers. Companions are judged on continuity. That creates pressure in three places at once:

Every consumer companion is a different set of trade-offs across those three constraints, and the trade-offs are increasingly how the apps are priced.

The context window is the total number of tokens the model can see when it writes the next reply. In a companion app, that budget is spent before the user says anything:

When the chat log outgrows what is left, the oldest messages fall out. The user experiences this as the companion "forgetting" mid-conversation. This is why context size is now a headline spec in the category, and a paid feature. SpicyChat AI, for example, gives free accounts a 4K context window, then scales memory to 8K on its $19.95/mo tier and 32K on its $29.95/mo tier. Janitor AI runs about 9K of context on its own JanitorLLM. Independent uncensored AI chat benchmarks record these context sizes alongside refusal behavior and pricing, because context length predicts how long a roleplay stays coherent better than any marketing claim.

Some apps sell memory in messages rather than tokens. Fantasy GF's pricing page lists a 60-message base "smart memory" and 5x that, 300 messages, on its Ultra plan. Others sell it as a memory multiplier on higher subscription tiers. The pattern is consistent across the category: memory depth is the upgrade lever, not the model itself.

No consumer app keeps an entire relationship in the prompt. Persistent memory is a separate system that writes to storage and reads back into the context window on each turn. In practice it has three layers:

The differences users notice are usually retention policies, not algorithms. Crushon AI's own subscription docs say free-plan chat history is deleted after 7 days of inactivity, while paid plans keep it for the membership period. Paid tiers across the category commonly promise that memories never expire. There is also a security angle worth stating plainly: companion chat logs are among the most sensitive data a consumer product can hold. A 2024 breach at Muah.ai, first reported by 404 Media, exposed roughly 1.9 million users' email addresses together with their private prompts. Any app that builds persistent memory is also building a liability, and developers copying the pattern should treat the memory store as the most protected part of the system.

A voice call in a companion app is three models chained together: speech-to-text, the LLM, and text-to-speech. Run them strictly in sequence and a single reply can take several seconds, which reads as dead air. The apps that feel fast attack each stage the same way:

With every stage streaming, time-to-first-audio can land close to a second on a good connection, which is roughly the threshold where a conversation stops feeling laggy. Voice is also expensive to run - three model calls instead of one, plus audio bandwidth - which is why it is metered harder than text. Fantasy GF charges 8 tokens per minute for voice calls (4 on Ultra), which turns the 100 monthly tokens on its entry plan into about 12 minutes of call time. SpicyChat, for its part, only adds text-to-speech at its top tier. Latency engineering and unit economics are the same problem here: faster pipelines cost less per call, and cheaper calls are what make voice viable below the top plan.

Tier details below come from each app's own pricing or documentation pages, checked November 16, 2026, and cross-checked against il.ly's AI companion testing guide, which hands-tests 15 companion apps across memory, media, and voice.

Two takeaways from the table. First, nobody sells a bigger base model - they sell a bigger window into the same one. Second, the free tiers are deliberately memory-poor: limited context and short retention are the upgrade prompt.

Even if you never build a companion, these apps are a useful reference architecture for any stateful LLM product:

Consumer AI companions have turned three hard infrastructure problems - bounded context, durable memory, and real-time voice - into their pricing pages. Context size, memory depth, and voice access are the tiers. If you are building anything stateful on an LLM, this category is worth watching closely: it is solving continuity and latency problems at consumer scale, in public, one subscription tier at a time.

It is the total amount of text, measured in tokens, that the model can consider when writing its next reply. The character definition, stored memories, recent chat, and the new message all share that budget. When the conversation exceeds it, the oldest content drops out and the companion appears to forget.

Yes, through a memory system separate from the context window. Apps summarize older chats, extract stable facts about you into a profile, and retrieve relevant past moments into the prompt. Retention varies: some free tiers delete chat history after as little as 7 days of inactivity, while paid plans typically keep memory for the membership period.

A voice turn runs three models - speech-to-text, the LLM, and text-to-speech - instead of one, plus audio transfer. Providers pass that cost on through per-minute token charges or by locking voice behind higher tiers.

For casual chat, 4K to 9K tokens works. For long-running roleplay or a companion expected to hold plot threads for weeks, 32K and up, or a well-built memory layer on top of a smaller window, makes the visible difference.

#10233. Clicks Communicator hits a supply chain snag, and it isn’t because of RAM

Clicks is still on track to release the phone with a physical keyboard within the next few months, but a Communicator launch before the end of this year isn’t looking very likely as shipping gets pushed back.

The Communicator is a fresh take on what an Android phone can be. It was first introduced at CES earlier this year, with the main selling angle being the physically integrated keyboard. Clicks wants to target those who are looking for a second device, and the Communicator fits the bill.

Last we heard, the Clicks Communicator was on track to launch and ship in December, following a cheery update detailing an upgrade to 12GB of RAM and other changes. However, Clicks posted a new update today stating that the timeline has shifted out a month because of supply chain issues caused by something other than RAM.

Unfortunately, we’ve encountered a delay with securing a critical supply chain component needed to fabricate our printed circuit board ahead of manufacturing and final assembly. Assembly will now begin in early January, with deliveries expected to start in late January.

It notes that the “critical supply chain component” is the essential fabric used for the company’s printed circuit boards. The blog post dives into some neat details about the component holding things up. The PCBs Clicks is using are made up of more material layers than some competitor boards. But the fabric used to layer those boards is in short supply at this sort of demand, with four manufacturers reportedly unable to meet the company’s required volume.

The post also spells out difficulties caused by troubles in the Middle East, and rising memory costs straining the chain of supply further, even if memory – surprisingly – isn’t the cause of this small delay. Those compounded issues have pushed production back, and users are now told to expect their Clicks Communicator pre-orders to be shipped in late January, at the earliest. Many pre-orders will likely be shipped after that because of further time constraints before Chinese New Year.

In an act of good faith, Clicks says it’s including a set of 3.5mm wired headphones in each box for orders placed prior to today.

A push from December to January isn’t massive, and Clicks still seems to be making solid time on developing a form factor we don’t often see. You can still pre-order the Clicks Communicator starting at $649.

'They're making so much money that don't know what to do with it.'

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

President Donald Trump, who has been pushing for AI development in the U.S., said that he wants to prevent U.S. companies from building data centers in other countries or charge them with a “tremendous tax” if they push ahead with their overseas plans. The move comes as a surprise, Trump said, as he hasn’t even discussed the proposed policy with his cabinet and advisors, reports Axios.

“They’re making so much money they don’t know what to do with it,” the U.S. president said. He added that Americans should negotiate with AI hyperscalers that want to establish data centers in communities, as they could help reduce costs and taxes. “They have plenty of money. Take it,” Trump added.

Several states and jurisdictions have started implementing similar solutions, with one Pennsylvania town listing 43 specific demands to approve an AI data center, while New York State recommended the municipalities demand a $1 million community investment per megawatt. Some developers embraced this, with one offering $10,000 per household if its 1,3000-acre facility gets approved, while Elon Musk’s SpaceX is buying out 418 homes near its flagship AI data center.

This is going to be an issue for many Americans, though, especially as AI data center measures put forward in the recently concluded mid-term elections have been comprehensively defeated. In fact, data center moratoriums have already surged past 500 across the U.S., making it harder for developers to push forward with their projects.

One possible reason Trump is considering a ban on overseas data centers for U.S. companies is that Chinese AI labs have been exploiting a loophole in U.S. law. This technicality has allowed them to export data to overseas data centers that use AI chips banned for export to China, like Nvidia’s Blackwell and upcoming Vera Rubin chips. “We’re not allowing data centers to go into other countries because China will take all of them, and we’re not allowing that to happen,” Trump said.

The U.S. House of Representatives passed the Remote Access Security Act (RASA) in January 2026 to close the workaround, but the bill has been in limbo in the Senate since then. The U.S. Bureau of Industry and Security (BIS) is also taking steps to prevent this, with Nvidia warning its clients that their customers should be at least 51% not from China to avoid legal exposure.

Another question is how the Trump administration will implement its “tremendous tax.” Congress is the only federal body with the constitutional power to impose taxes, and with the opposing party controlling both the House and the Senate, it would be doubly hard for the White House to implement its legislative priorities without serious concessions.

Get Tom's Hardware's best news and in-depth reviews, straight to your inbox.

Finally, there’s the issue of data sovereignty and latency. U.S. companies would build data centers in foreign countries because some governments demand that their data remain within their territory. So, if Washington proceeds with the ban, American tech companies could potentially lose this market. Aside from that, keeping data centers in the U.S. would result in longer latency and slower responses, making it harder to serve customers located on the other side of the globe.

#10229. Google retiring Nest app and Google Assistant on speakers, displays in 2027

Following the Android deprecation in September, Google Assistant is going away on speakers and smart displays next year. Additionally, Google Home is fully replacing the Nest app in January.

The Nest app on Android, iOS, and the web will stop working on January 25, 2027. The Google Home app, which requires a Google Account, will be needed to “continue controlling and receiving notifications from your Nest devices remotely.”

Officially, Google wants to “focus all of [its] engineering, security, and feature investments on one unified, modern experience.” It reiterated benefits like:

With home.nest.com going away, you have to download any custom video clips since they will not transfer over to Google Home. A one-click “Download All” button is available.

Terrible news. For all my first-gen Nest cameras (wired hello doorbell, wired outdoor cams, and multiple indoor cams), I vastly prefer the Nest app to the terrible Google Home app.

Otherwise, Nest app users do not need to do anything else, with devices automatically transferring over to the Google Home app. No Nest thermostats, cameras, doorbells, smoke alarms, or locks are losing support with this change.

Additionally, Nest Aware (1st Gen) subscriptions will automatically switch to Google Home Premium “with no additional cost and no credit card re-entry required at this time.”

The other change announced today is that Google Assistant on speakers and smart displays is going away on August 2, 2027. It will be replaced by the Gemini for Home voice assistant. Google notes that natural language support replaces “rigid, static and highly specific voice commands.” Additionally:

You can upgrade to Gemini today, while Google is expanding international availability over the coming months. Google will share more updates closer to the deadline.
